# **2\. Introduction**

## **2.1 Problem Definition**

Short-form video is the dominant content format on the internet, mediating a creator economy valued at approximately $250 billion \[1\]. Yet this ecosystem is controlled by three centralized platforms: TikTok (1.6–1.9B monthly active users), YouTube Shorts (2B+), and Instagram Reels (2B) \[2\], \[3\], \[4\]. All three platforms suffer from opaque algorithms, exploitative creator economics, and data practices that create systemic risks for all participants. Additionally, TikTok's ability to operate in the US has been under threat, and the current deal still gives China-based ByteDance oversight over data and algorithms \[5\], \[6\].

The AT Protocol, with 43 million users and a maturing developer ecosystem \[7\], provides a technical foundation for decentralized alternatives. Skylight, a short-form video app built on AT Protocol, has demonstrated market demand with 380,000+ users since April 2025 and introduced a compelling human curation model where users build and share thematic video feeds \[8\]. However, Skylight and similar efforts are constrained by a deeper architectural limitation: the interaction primitives available on AT Protocol, namely likes, reposts, and follows, carry no video-specific information. Even Skylight's recommendation engine has no access to watch duration, completion rate, rewatch behavior, or sound engagement, because that data does not exist at the protocol level.

OpenReel addresses three critical gaps in the AT Protocol video ecosystem:

* **Signal (data layer).** No video-specific interaction data exists on AT Protocol. A “like” carries no information about whether the user watched 3 seconds or 3 minutes, whether sound was on, or whether the content was consumed in a video-first context. OpenReel will define new Lexicon schemas for video engagement records, creating the data layer that video discovery requires. With individual behavioral data owned by users on their PDS, and only anonymized aggregate signals available to feed generators by default.

* **Discovery (application layer).** Users need the ability to choose how signal data is used to curate their experience. OpenReel will build a feed generator ecosystem where algorithms are transparent about their ranking criteria, and users can select, swap, or unsubscribe from feeds rather than being locked into a single opaque recommendation engine.

* **Experience (frontend layer).** Bluesky's playback infrastructure was designed for occasional video embeds, not rapid full-screen sequential consumption. Users of existing AT Protocol video apps report audio bleed between videos during fast scrolling, buffering interruptions, and inconsistent playback state, which are symptoms of a text-first architecture repurposed for a video-first use case.

A fourth gap compounds these technical problems: **content safety**. Bluesky's labeler system works for text, but unlabeled video uploaded to a PDS can surface in feeds without automated classification, leaving viewers without a warning layer between the firehose and their playback. OpenReel will implement safe-by-default feeds where video must pass automated classification before entering discovery: not moderating the protocol, but moderating what OpenReel surfaces.

### **2.1.1 Who Is Affected, and How?**

**Creators** have no control over how their content is discovered, no ability to take their audience with them, and no recourse when platforms change the rules. Algorithmic changes can destroy a creator’s reach overnight without explanation; shadowbanning is widely reported on TikTok and Instagram, yet neither platform offers meaningful transparency or appeals \[9\]. Cross-posting is actively penalized: Instagram suppresses Reels with TikTok watermarks from recommendation, and vice versa \[10\]. When TikTok shut down, creators discovered they could not transfer followers, engagement history, or community connections anywhere \[11\] and pivoting to another centralized platform merely transfers the same dependency. The economic stakes are significant (the creator economy is valued at $250 billion \[1\]), but even well-compensated creators operate on rented infrastructure where the landlord can change the terms at any time.

**Consumers** face algorithmic filter bubbles (a 2025 PNAS Nexus study found engagement algorithms amplify hostile content users don’t actually prefer), addictive design (the EU ruled TikTok’s UX breaches the Digital Services Act in February 2026 \[12\]), and systemic privacy violations (the FTC’s 2024 report found “vast surveillance” across major platforms \[13\]). Average daily TikTok usage has reached 95 minutes \[14\]. Only 14% of Gen Z fully trust social platforms to handle their personal information responsibly, and users have zero ability to choose or replace the algorithm governing what they see \[15\].

### **2.1.2 Why Does It Need to Be Solved Now?**

**Platform fragility is proven.** The TikTok shutdown and subsequent year of operation under executive non-enforcement orders \[11\] demonstrated that any single-entity platform can be rendered inaccessible by geopolitical forces outside the creator-platform relationship. The mass migration to Xiaohongshu (\#tiktokrefugee) \[16\] and the scramble to redirect audiences to Instagram and YouTube exposed in real time that creator audiences are not portable across centralized platforms.

**AT Protocol** is technically ready. Video support launched September 2024, expanded to 3-minute uploads in March 2025 \[17\]. The custom feed system hosts 50,000+ community-built algorithms \[18\]. The Lexicon system enables application-specific record types. Bluesky’s $100M Series B closed April 2025 \[19\]. IETF standardization began November 2025 \[20\]. Skylight’s 380,000 users validate demand, but the design space for video-native protocol infrastructure remains wide open.

**Regulation is moving toward decentralized principles.** The EU Digital Markets Act mandates data portability from gatekeepers \[21\]. The EU’s TikTok ruling puts platform UX within regulatory scope. In the U.S., KOSA requires algorithmic transparency \[22\]; KOSMA would ban recommendation algorithms for minors \[23\]; 45+ states have introduced 300+ bills on social media algorithms \[24\]. Systems built on AT Protocol are compliant by design where centralized platforms face costly retrofitting.

The technical foundation exists, market demand is validated, and the regulatory environment is favorable. OpenReel’s contribution is to build what doesn’t yet exist: a video-native discovery, engagement, and safety layer on AT Protocol, giving creators ownership of their audience and consumers meaningful choice over how they experience short-form video.

## **2.2 Problem Resolution**

Our proposed solution is a modular, decentralized short-form video platform. Instead of one company dictating the algorithm and content guidelines, our platform distributes that control across the people who actually create and consume the content. Hence, it will serve as a modular template for a decentralized social media topology, allowing users to substitute our components for ones that fit their needs. Independent developers will be able to create custom video recommendation algorithms that connect with our network’s media and metadata servers. Users may use different frontend apps and websites to watch the videos, which can be pulled from configurable sources.

The platform will be built on the AT Protocol, the same decentralized social networking foundation behind Bluesky. This gives us a proven base for identity management, data portability, and federation without requiring us to design a new protocol from scratch. Users authenticate through decentralized identifiers (DIDs), meaning their identity isn't tied to any single service provider and can move with them across frontends and servers. Additionally, using the AT Protocol allows us to use seed data from other platforms, helping alleviate the chicken-or-egg problem inherent in most social media platforms: the first users would have no content to watch and no creators would publish videos without a substantial user base. Since other platforms built on the AT Protocol support videos, we can use them to bootstrap our content.

For media storage and distribution, the platform will provide a default hosted service so that users can upload and share videos without any technical setup. However, because content is stored and referenced through AT Protocol personal data servers (PDS) the system natively supports federation, as users can point to a third-party PDS provider or self-host if they choose. Video content and its associated metadata (e.g., captions, tags, and engagement data) will be stored as AT Protocol records, making them portable across providers. If a user decides to leave our hosted service, they can migrate their entire profile to another compatible server without losing their content, followers, or identity.

The recommendation system will be designed as an open, pluggable interface rather than a built-in feature of the platform itself. We define a standardized API that algorithm servers implement to receive content metadata from the network and return ranked video feeds. Any developer can build and deploy their own algorithm server, choosing whether it prioritizes chronological ordering, niche interest communities, local creators, or novel ML ranking strategies. Users select which algorithm servers curate their feed and can switch between them freely.

Our primary deliverable is a native iOS application for recording, uploading, browsing, and watching short-form video. However, because the media and algorithm layers communicate through open protocol interfaces, the iOS app is just one possible client. The modular architecture means third-party developers can build alternative frontends, all connecting to the same content network and algorithm servers. Our iOS app serves as the reference implementation that proves the architecture works end to end.

## **2.3 Benefits of Solution**

### **2.3.1 Platform-Level Benefits**

| Reduced Centralized Control | By leveraging the AT Protocol, the platform shifts power away from corporations and redistributes it to users and developers, reducing the risk of monopolistic control over content distribution. |
| :---- | :---- |
| **Greater Transparency in Algorithms** | An open, pluggable recommendation system allows society to better understand how feed content is curated, reducing hidden bias or manipulation. |
| **Encourages Innovation** | A decentralized modular ecosystem enables smaller independent developers to build new features, algorithms, and services on top of the same network, fostering competition and innovation. |

### **2.3.2 Benefits to End Users**

| Data Ownership and Portability | Users would retain ownership of their identities and content through decentralized unique identifiers. They would be able to switch platforms or providers without losing any of their data, followers, or history. |
| :---- | :---- |
| **Customizable User Experience** | Users wont be locked into a single recommendation algorithm, they can choose or switch between different feed algorithms based on their preferences, whether it is simply chronological (how recent the post was made), interest-based, or community-driven (most popular posts). |
| **Increased Privacy and Control** | Since users can choose their own personal data server (PDS) or even self-host, they gain far more control over where their data is stored as well as how it is managed. |
| **Platform Flexibility** | Users are no longer tied to a single app, multiple frontends (including the flagship OpenReel iOS app) can access the same content, giving users the freedom to choose interfaces that best suit their needs. |

### **2.3.3 Secondary and Indirect Benefits**

| Support for Research and Experimentation | The platform would provide an open environment for researchers, students, and independent developers to design and test new recommendation algorithms on real-world data. This would lower the barrier for experimentation and enable practical evolution of things like machine learning approaches in a live social media context. |
| :---- | :---- |
| **Reduced Platform Lock-in** | Content creators would no longer be dependent on any one platform for visibility or income, they would be able to migrate from one platform to another freely without having to rebuild their audiences from scratch. |
| **Healthier Content Ecosystems** | With multiple different open-source algorithms available, users are not forced into a single opaque feed, this would allow them to explore different perspectives while also retaining the option to tailor their experiences, thus reducing reliance on any one potentially biased system. |

#   **3\. Requirements**

## **3.1 Hardware Requirements**

The hardware requirements for OpenReel are based on the needs of the iOS development environment, the end-user device, and the cloud infrastructure supporting the platform's backend services.

### **3.1.1 End-User Device (iOS Client)**

| Specification | Minimum | Recommended |
| ----- | ----- | ----- |
| Device | iPhone 12 or later | iPhone 14 or later |
| OS | iOS 16.0 or later | iOS 17.0 or later |
| RAM | 4 GB | 6 GB or more |
| Storage | 500 MB (app \+ local cache) | 2 GB or more |
| Network | Wi-Fi or 4G LTE cellular | Wi-Fi or 5G cellular |
| Camera | Required for in-app video recording | Supports higher resolution recording |

### **3.1.2 Development Hardware**

| Specification | Minimum | Recommended |
| ----- | ----- | ----- |
| Computer | Mac running macOS 13 (Ventura) or later (required for Xcode) | Mac with Apple Silicon (M2 or later) |
| CPU | Apple Silicon (M1) or Intel x86\_64 | Apple Silicon (M2 or later) |
| RAM | 8 GB | 16 GB or more |
| Storage | 20 GB available (Xcode, simulators, project files) | 100 GB or more |
| Physical Device | Recommended for on-device testing (video, audio, camera) | Required for camera/playback testing |

### **3.1.3 Server / Cloud Infrastructure**

| Component | Description |
| ----- | ----- |
| **Media Storage** | Cloud object storage (AWS S3 or equivalent) for hosted video files and metadata |
| **Content Delivery Network (CDN)** | Required for low-latency video streaming across geographic regions |
| **Application Servers** | Cloud-hosted servers to handle API requests between the iOS client, PDS, and feed generator servers |
| **Personal Data Server (PDS)** | AT Protocol-compatible server for user identity, records, and content metadata |
| **Feed Generator Servers** | Independently hosted servers implementing the standardized feed generator API |

## **3.2 User / Functional Requirements**

### **3.2.1 Feature List**

| Feature | User Story | Acceptance Criteria |
| ----- | ----- | ----- |
| Video Recording | As a creator, I would like to record short-form videos directly in the app so I can capture and share content without switching to another tool. | A user can open the app, record using the device camera and microphone, preview the recording, and proceed to publish without leaving the app. |
| Video Upload | As a creator, I would like to upload pre-recorded video from my camera roll so that I can share content I have already filmed. | A user can select a video from their camera roll, attach captions and tags, and successfully submit it for publication. |
| Video Feed / Browse | As a viewer, I want to scroll through a full-screen feed of short-form videos so that I can discover and consume content seamlessly. | A user can scroll through a continuous full-screen feed with smooth transitions, consistent audio, and no playback interruptions between clips. |
| Video Playback | As a viewer, I want videos to play automatically and without interruption so that my viewing experience is uninterrupted. | The app buffers content in advance, prevents audio bleed between videos, and handles network interruptions gracefully. |
| Algorithm Selection | As a user, I want to choose and switch between different feed algorithm servers so that I have control over how my content is curated. | A user can view available feed generators, read each one's ranking criteria, select one, and see their feed update in real time without restarting the app. |
| Decentralized Authentication | As a user, I want to log in using a DID so that my account is not tied to any single platform. | A user can create a new DID or log in with an existing one, with their account not dependent on OpenReel's servers to authenticate. |
| Account Portability / Migration | As a user, I want to migrate my account, content, and followers to different PDS providers so that I am never locked into OpenReel's hosted service. | A user can initiate an account migration and have their profile, content, followers, and engagement data transfer completely with their DID unchanged. |
| Content Safety Filtering | As a viewer, I want videos to be automatically screened before appearing in my feed so that I am not exposed to explicit or harmful content without warning. | No video appears in any user's feed without first passing automated content classification; harmful content is withheld from discovery by default. |

### **3.2.2 Use Cases**

#### **3.2.2.1 Use Case: Creator Uploads Their First Video**

User is a content creator who wants to share short term videos with their audience. They download OpenReel and create an account using a decentralized identifier, meaning that their identity belongs to them, not the platform. They open the app, hit record, and capture a short clip directly without needing to switch to another app. They add captions and tags to help people discover their content, then hit publish. Before the video reaches any feed, OpenReel’s content classification system automatically screens it. Once approved, the video and all its metadata are stored as AT Protocol records on their own personal data server. Unlike TikTok or Instagram, the user owns their content from day one. And if they leave OpenReel, they take everything with them.

#### **3.2.2.2 Use Case: Viewer Customizes Their Feed Algorithm**

A user frustrated with TikTok's recommendation system opens OpenReel and navigates to feed settings. They see a list of available algorithm servers, each displaying their ranking criteria: one prioritizes completion rate, another weighs local creators, another is purely chronological. The user selects the chronological feed to start and later switches to the local creator feed. Their experience updates in real time with no restart required.

#### **3.2.2.3 Use Case: Creator Migrates Their Account**

A creator who has been posting on OpenReel's hosted service for six months wants more control over their data and decides to self-host their own personal data server. Through account settings, they initiate a migration. Their entire profile, video history, followers, and engagement data transfer seamlessly. Their DID remains the same, so followers don't lose them and all existing links to their content continue to work.

### **3.2.3 Agreed-Upon Scope**

The primary deliverable for OpenReel is a native iOS application that supports video recording, uploading, browsing, and playback built on the AT Protocol. The team has additionally committed to implementing a default hosted personal data server, a standardized feed generator API supporting pluggable recommendation algorithms, and a safe-by-default content classification system.

Alternative frontends, third-party algorithm servers, and self-hosted PDS configurations are supported by the architecture but are not deliverables owned by this team. The iOS app serves as the reference implementation demonstrating the full system working end to end.

## **3.3 Non-User / Non-Functional Requirements**

### **3.3.1 Performance**

| Feed Loading | Fast feed loading times; AT Protocol was explicitly designed to prioritize performance at large scale. |
| :---- | :---- |
| **API Response Time** | Under 500ms for fetching posts, follows, and likes under normal load. |
| **Uptime** | 99.9% or better; graceful handling of PDS unavailability since any individual server in the federated network can go offline at any time. |
| **WebSocket Stability** | Connections to the relay/firehose must be stable and reconnect automatically on drops to ensure real-time data stays in sync. |

### **3.3.2 Security**

| Identity Ownership | User identity must be cryptographically owned by the user via DIDs: no single company, including Bluesky, can revoke a user's identity without their involvement. |
| :---- | :---- |
| **Cryptographic Signing** | All data written to a user's repository must be cryptographically signed; the application must validate signatures to prevent impersonation or data tampering. |
| **Dual-Key Model** | The system must support both a signing key (used daily) and a recovery key (held by the user), following AT Protocol's dual-key model. |
| **Authentication** | User authentication must follow AT Protocol's OAuth implementation standards. |
| **Encryption in Transit** | All data in transit must be encrypted via HTTPS and secure WebSockets (WSS). |

### **3.3.3 Scalability and Maintainability**

| Firehose Throughput | The application must handle high-throughput event streams from AT Protocol's relay firehose, which aggregates activity across all PDS instances on the network. |
| :---- | :---- |
| **Modular Microservices** | The architecture must use AT Protocol's modular microservice model, separating PDS, relay, AppView, and feed generator concerns, so individual components can be scaled independently. |
| **Lexicon Schema Compliance** | The codebase must use AT Protocol's Lexicon schema system for all data types, ensuring new features can be added without breaking compatibility with other clients. |
| **Account Migration Support** | If a user's PDS changes, the app must continue to resolve their identity and content correctly without data loss. |

### **3.3.4 Compatibility and Platform Requirements**

| PDS Compatibility | The application must be compatible with any AT Protocol-compliant PDS, not just Bluesky-hosted servers. |
| :---- | :---- |
| **API Protocol** | All API communication must use XRPC over HTTP with JSON, as specified by AT Protocol. |
| **Interoperability** | Data created in this app must be readable by any other compliant AT Protocol application. |
| **CBOR Encoding** | Since AT Protocol data is encoded in CBOR format for authenticated content, the backend must support proper encoding/decoding. |

### **3.3.5 Legal and Compliance**

| GDPR / CCPA | The application must comply with GDPR and CCPA, ensuring users are informed about what data is collected and shared. |
| :---- | :---- |
| **Public Data Disclosure** | The application must clearly disclose that user posts, likes, and follows are publicly readable by any AT Protocol client or relay by default. |
| **Data Deletion** | Users must have the ability to request deletion of their data from the application. |
| **Identity Non-Revocability** | The application must respect that user identities (DIDs) are owned by the user and cannot be revoked or reassigned by the platform. |
| **Moderation Policy** | The application must have a documented content moderation policy that clarifies what content is filtered or flagged. |

#   **4\. Project-Related Research**

## **4.1 Research Topic: Federated AT Protocol Design**

### **4.1.1 Introduction**

One of the first fundamental questions OpenReel has to answer is what protocol to build on. Since the whole point of the project is to give users control over their identity and content, the underlying protocol needs to support this by design. This research topic looks at the AT Protocol (Authenticated Transfer Protocol), developed by Bluesky Social PBC, and makes the case that it is the right foundation for OpenReel. The AT Protocol's decentralized identity system, modular architecture, and extensible Lexicon schema system make it the most practical and well-suited base for a video-first federated platform like ours.

### **4.1.2 Problem Statement**

Short-form video platforms today are fully centralized, meaning the platform owns the user's identity, content, and social graph. Users have no real portability, no say in how their feed is curated, and no recourse when the platform changes the rules. Existing decentralized protocols were not built with video in mind and lack the schema primitives and engagement signal types that a video-native platform requires. OpenReel needs to identify which federated protocol best solves these problems while still being something a student team can realistically build on.

### **4.1.3 Research**

#### **4.1.3.1 The AT Protocol: Architecture and Core Components**

The AT Protocol is a decentralized, schema-driven architecture built for feed-based social media. Its main goal is to take power over identity, content, and moderation away from centralized corporations and return it to users and developers through a modular microservice design where no single entity controls the whole stack \[25\].

The protocol is built around a few key components. The Personal Data Server (PDS) is where a user's data lives: it handles identity, content storage, and federation, and can be self-hosted or run by a third-party provider. Every user is identified by a Decentralized Identifier (DID), a cryptographic identifier that stays with the user even if they switch providers \[26\]. Network Relays crawl all the PDSes in the network and stream updates in real time through the firehose. Feed generators and App Views subscribe to this firehose to build feeds and application-level experiences \[27\].

One idea that is central to the protocol's design is the separation of speech and reach. The speech layer (the PDS and user repositories) is permissive and distributed: anyone can publish. The reach layer (relays, feed generators, and App Views) is where curation happens, and it can be run by independent third parties. This means users are not stuck with one algorithm; they can pick their own aggregators \[28\].

#### **4.1.3.2 Decentralized Identity (DIDs) and Account Portability**

The identity model is one of the strongest parts of AT Protocol. A user's handle resolves to a DID, which points to a document containing their public key and current PDS location. Any changes to that document must be signed with the user's private key, creating a verifiable chain of updates. Importantly, users can change their handle without losing their identity because the DID stays the same \[26\].

This is what the AT Protocol calls a credible exit. When a user migrates their account, their full history, social graph, and identity keypair all move with them without data loss \[29\]. If a PDS goes offline, the user can migrate to a new provider by updating their DID Document and uploading a backup. Their followers do not lose them and all existing links to their content still work.

Security-wise, the protocol uses a dual-key model: a signing key for daily use and a recovery key held by the user for account recovery. Authentication follows AT Protocol's OAuth standards, and all data in transit is encrypted via HTTPS and secure WebSockets \[30\].

#### **4.1.3.3 The Lexicon Schema System and Protocol Extensions**

Lexicon is AT Protocol's schema definition language. Every record type and API endpoint in the protocol is defined by a Lexicon schema written in JSON, identified by a Namespaced Identifier (NSID) in reverse-DNS format. This is what makes different AT Protocol apps able to understand each other's data \[31\].

The key thing for OpenReel is that Lexicon is designed to be extended. Any developer who controls a domain can publish new Lexicons under that namespace without needing permission from Bluesky. New record schemas, API endpoints, and event stream types can all be defined this way \[32\]. For OpenReel, this means defining custom record types like io.openreel.video.post, io.openreel.engagement.watchEvent, and io.openreel.engagement.completionRate to capture video-specific signals that do not exist anywhere in the base Bluesky schemas. These records would live on users' own PDSes, keeping data ownership with the user while still allowing feed generators to access anonymized aggregate signals.

#### **4.1.3.4 Current AT Protocol Ecosystem Maturity**

By early 2026, the AT Protocol has matured significantly. Core parts of the protocol were submitted to the IETF for standardization in late 2025, and a working group charter was published in January 2026 \[33\]. Bluesky closed a $100M Series B in April 2025, held its first protocol-focused conference in March 2025, and the custom feed system now hosts over 50,000 community-built algorithms \[17\].

Skylight, a short-form video app built on AT Protocol, crossed 380,000 users after launching in April 2025\. This proves the protocol can support video apps in production. It also confirms what OpenReel's problem statement identifies: the protocol-level engagement data layer for video is still missing, and that is the gap this project is specifically designed to fill.

### **4.1.4 Alternate Methods**

#### **4.1.4.1 ActivityPub (The Fediverse)**

ActivityPub is a W3C-recommended standard from 2018 and the most widely used decentralized social protocol today. It powers Mastodon, PeerTube, Pixelfed, and others. Servers can federate with each other, and PeerTube shows it can handle video at scale. The problem for OpenReel is data ownership: in ActivityPub, the instance operator controls the user's data. When a user switches instances, their past posts cannot be imported to the new one \[34\]. AT Protocol's credible exit mechanism handles portability more directly \[35\].

#### **4.1.4.2 Nostr**

Nostr (Notes and Other Stuff Transmitted by Relays) is a relay-based protocol built around censorship resistance. Users hold cryptographic keypairs instead of server-tied usernames, and messages are broadcast to multiple relays simultaneously \[36\]. The tradeoff is usability and discoverability: relays do not communicate with each other, there is no equivalent to AT Protocol's firehose for aggregating content, and there is no standardized schema extension system like Lexicon. For a video-first app that needs structured engagement data, Nostr does not provide the right foundation.

#### **4.1.4.3 Farcaster**

Farcaster anchors identity on-chain using Ethereum. Every account has a unique Farcaster ID managed by a smart contract, and users register by making an Ethereum transaction \[37\]. This gives very strong identity sovereignty guarantees since no server operator can revoke an account. For OpenReel, the blockchain dependency is more complex than the use case demands; it adds transaction costs for registration, creates an Ethereum infrastructure dependency, and brings in concerns not relevant to building a short-form video app \[35\].

#### **4.1.4.4 Building a Custom Protocol**

A fourth option would be designing a protocol from scratch optimized entirely for short-form video. This would give maximum flexibility but would mean solving problems AT Protocol has already solved: identity, federation, relay infrastructure, and data portability. It would also create a cold-start problem since a brand new protocol would have no existing users or content, whereas AT Protocol lets OpenReel bootstrap from the existing Bluesky network.

### **4.1.5 Comparison of Methods**

| Method | Pros | Cons |
| ----- | ----- | ----- |
| AT Protocol | Cryptographic user-owned identity (DIDs); true account portability/migration; extensible Lexicon schemas for custom video records; active ecosystem (Bluesky, Skylight); IETF standardization in progress; existing content for seeding; modular microservice architecture | Most federation currently runs through Bluesky infrastructure in practice; credible exit requires technical knowledge; video-specific engagement data layer not yet standardized; newer than ActivityPub |
| ActivityPub | Most widely adopted decentralized protocol; W3C recommended standard; mature ecosystem (Mastodon, PeerTube); proven for video via PeerTube; largest existing user base | User data owned by server operator, not user; past posts cannot be ported between instances; no built-in equivalent to Lexicon for app-specific schemas; not designed for single global social graph |
| Nostr | True censorship resistance; purely client-side key ownership; no server dependency; no central identity authority | No standardized schema extension system; relays do not federate with each other; poor discoverability; small ecosystem; not well suited for rich media apps |
| Farcaster | Strongest identity sovereignty (on-chain); cannot be revoked by any operator; growing developer ecosystem | Ethereum transaction costs for identity; blockchain infrastructure dependency; limited video-specific tooling; smaller ecosystem; overkill for this use case |
| Custom Protocol | Maximum flexibility; fully optimized for video; no constraints from existing schemas | Enormous engineering effort; bootstrapping problem (no existing users or content); no existing relay/PDS infrastructure; reinvents solved problems |

### **4.1.6 Application to OpenReel**

#### **4.1.6.1 Selected Method: AT Protocol**

AT Protocol is the right choice for OpenReel. The DID-based identity system is what makes the platform's core promise possible: users actually own their accounts. The credible exit mechanism means a user can leave OpenReel's hosted PDS and move to a self-hosted or third-party PDS without losing anything, which directly maps to the account portability requirement in the project specification.

The Lexicon extension system is what really seals it. OpenReel needs video-specific engagement records that do not exist in Bluesky's default schemas, and Lexicon provides a structured, interoperable way to define them. By publishing custom schemas under the io.openreel.\* namespace, the team can create record types for watch events, completion rates, and rewatch behavior that live on users' PDSes and can be consumed by feed generators. The existing ecosystem also helps practically, as Skylight's 380,000+ users and existing AT Protocol video infrastructure mean there is already content on the network to bootstrap from.

#### **4.1.6.2 Implementation Considerations**

Authentication will use AT Protocol's OAuth implementation. Users will be able to bring an existing DID or create one during onboarding, and the iOS app will communicate through XRPC over HTTP with JSON, as the protocol specifies.

For video records, the team should define schemas under a domain they control, such as io.openreel.\*, following the AT Protocol Lexicon style guide. Fields should use lowerCamelCase, use knownValues instead of closed enums so schemas stay extensible, and be designed for forward compatibility since records stored across the network cannot be retroactively updated.

The feed generator layer should be a standardized API that any algorithm server can implement, consistent with AT Protocol's open feed generator model. This lets the team ship a working default feed while leaving the door open for third-party developers to build their own algorithms, which is how OpenReel fulfills the Discovery requirement without having to own every possible ranking approach.

Content safety will operate at the AppView layer, consistent with AT Protocol's speech vs. reach separation. Videos can be published freely to the protocol, but OpenReel's AppView only surfaces content that has passed automated classification. This is the right architectural place for that filter, and it does not require modifying the protocol itself.

## **4.2 Research Topic: Recommendation Algorithms for Decentralized Short-Form Video**

### **4.2.1 Introduction**

A core architectural decision of OpenReel is how to design and support recommendation algorithms within a federated, user-controlled ecosystem. Unlike centralized platforms such as TikTok, YouTube Shorts, and Instagram Reels, where a single opaque algorithm governs what every user sees, OpenReel’s pluggable feed generator architecture allows users to choose between multiple transparent ranking systems. **This research investigates which families of recommendation algorithms are most appropriate for a decentralized short-form video platform, what engagement signals must be captured to support them, and how OpenReel’s privacy-tiered data architecture constrains and shapes the design space.**

### **4.2.2 Problem Statement**

The AT Protocol currently lacks video-specific interaction data, and no standardized framework exists for deciding which recommendation approaches are feasible, effective, and aligned with user autonomy in a decentralized short-form video context. Existing AT Protocol interaction primitives (likes, reposts, follows) are binary signals designed for microblogging. They carry no information about watch duration, completion rate, sound engagement, or other behavioral signals that are essential for video recommendation. Skylight, the most prominent AT Protocol video app with over 380,000 users, has implemented basic collaborative filtering but operates without access to granular viewing behavior because that data does not exist at the protocol level. Meanwhile, centralized platforms optimize for engagement using deep learning systems like ByteDance’s Monolith architecture, which processes thousands of behavioral signals in real time. OpenReel must determine which algorithm families to support as default feeds, what new Lexicon schemas to define for video engagement records, and how to structure data access so that increasingly sophisticated algorithms become available as users opt in to sharing richer engagement data.

### **4.2.3 Research**

#### **4.2.3.1 How Centralized Platforms Approach Video Recommendation**

Understanding how TikTok’s recommendation system works provides essential context for what OpenReel is building against. TikTok’s For You Page is driven by an algorithm that analyzes user interactions (watches, likes, shares, comments, skips), video metadata (captions, hashtags, sounds), and device/account settings to deliver hyper-personalized feeds. A leaked 2021 internal document revealed the algorithm optimizes for four goals: user value, long-term user value, creator value, and platform value, with the overarching aim of maximizing watch time and engagement \[38\]. ByteDance’s published research describes Monolith, a real-time recommendation system with collision-free embedding tables that handles the sparsity and rapid concept drift inherent in user preference data \[39\]. The system adapts to shifting user preferences through continuous online training rather than periodic batch updates.

What makes TikTok architecturally distinct is its content-first rather than social-first design. Unlike Instagram or Facebook, which historically surface content from accounts a user follows, TikTok prioritizes predicted relevance regardless of the creator’s follower count or the viewer’s social connections \[40\]. This means a new creator with zero followers can reach a large audience if their content generates strong engagement signals. Julian McAuley, a computer science professor at UC San Diego, has argued that TikTok’s advantage lies not in algorithmic novelty but in its combination of massive data volume, highly engaged users, and a consumption context where users are receptive to algorithmic recommendations \[38\].

However, this engagement-maximizing approach carries documented harms. Milli et al. \[41\] conducted a preregistered algorithmic audit published in PNAS Nexus and found that, relative to a reverse-chronological baseline, Twitter’s engagement-based ranking algorithm amplifies emotionally charged, out-group hostile content that users report not actually preferring. The study found that ranking by users’ stated preferences instead of engagement metrics reduced angry, partisan, and hostile content, though it also risked reinforcing echo chambers. This finding is directly relevant to OpenReel’s design: it provides empirical evidence that engagement optimization and user satisfaction are not synonymous, and that offering users a choice of algorithms with different objective functions is not merely a philosophical position but an evidence-based design decision.

#### **4.2.3.2 Recommendation Algorithm Families**

The recommender systems literature identifies several foundational algorithm families, each with distinct data requirements, strengths, and failure modes. The following analysis evaluates each approach in the context of OpenReel’s decentralized architecture, where user data ownership and opt-in data sharing are first-order constraints.

##### Chronological Feeds

The simplest approach is a reverse-chronological feed that displays content in order of upload time with no ranking. This requires no user data whatsoever and offers perfect transparency: the ranking criterion is the timestamp. Chronological feeds serve as the natural baseline for any recommendation system and appeal to users who distrust algorithmic curation. The AT Protocol’s feed generator architecture already supports this trivially, as the Bluesky “Following” feed demonstrates. The primary limitation is scalability of user attention: as content volume grows, users are overwhelmed with low-relevance posts, and new creators without an existing follower base have no discovery pathway.

##### Heuristic / Popularity-Based Ranking

Heuristic ranking uses a weighted combination of aggregate engagement signals such as view count, completion rate, like-to-view ratio, or recency to surface trending or high-performing content. Critically, this approach requires no individual user data; it operates entirely on anonymized aggregates that OpenReel’s architecture already plans to make available to feed generators by default. A heuristic feed might be defined by publicly visible weights such as 0.4 × completion rate \+ 0.3 × like ratio \+ 0.3 × recency decay, making its ranking criteria fully transparent. This aligns with the Bluesky feed generator documentation, which describes building a “What’s Hot” feed by tracking likes per post from the firehose and returning posts that pass a threshold \[42\]. The main weakness is popularity feedback loops: already-popular content accumulates more views and engagement, making it harder for new or niche creators to surface.

##### 3.2.3.2.3 Collaborative Filtering

Collaborative filtering recommends content by identifying users with similar engagement patterns and suggesting videos that similar users enjoyed but the target user has not yet seen. IBM’s technical documentation distinguishes user-based collaborative filtering (grouping users by behavior similarity), item-based collaborative filtering (grouping items by how users interact with them), and model-based approaches that use matrix factorization or neural embeddings to learn latent preference factors \[43\]. Collaborative filtering is effective at surfacing novel content that a user would not have discovered through metadata-based approaches alone, because it leverages cross-user behavioral patterns rather than item features.

Skylight, OpenReel’s closest existing competitor in the AT Protocol video space, has already implemented basic collaborative filtering for its recommendation algorithm \[44\]. However, Skylight’s implementation is constrained by the same data limitation that OpenReel’s new Lexicon schemas aim to address: the only engagement signals currently available on AT Protocol are binary likes, reposts, and follows, which provide far less information than watch duration or completion rate. Collaborative filtering also suffers from the well-documented cold-start problem: it cannot make useful recommendations for new users who have no interaction history or for new content that no users have yet engaged with \[45\], \[43\]. In OpenReel’s architecture, collaborative filtering requires users to opt in to sharing their engagement data with the feed generator, since individual behavioral data is stored on the user’s PDS and not available to external services by default.

##### 3.2.3.2.4 Content-Based Filtering

Content-based filtering recommends videos with similar metadata (tags, captions, audio track, creator, duration) to videos the user has previously engaged with. Unlike collaborative filtering, it does not require data from other users, making it feasible even for users who have not opted in to sharing behavioral data with feed generators, the matching can happen locally on the user’s PDS or client. A comparative study by Liao et al. \[46\] found that content-based systems tend to produce higher user trust when recommendations are explained, because the reasoning (“you liked videos with this tag, here are more”) is intuitive. Content-based filtering also handles the user cold-start problem better than collaborative filtering, since even a small number of interactions can seed meaningful recommendations \[47\]. The main risk is filter bubbles: the system only recommends content similar to what the user has already seen, which can narrow the user’s exposure over time and suppress serendipitous discovery.

##### 3.2.3.2.5 Social / Graph-Based

Social graph-based ranking surfaces videos from creators within the user’s social network, weighted by connection proximity (first-degree follows, second-degree mutual connections, etc.). This approach is particularly natural for AT Protocol applications because the follow graph is public and already available to feed generators via the firehose without requiring any additional data opt-in. Skylight’s 2.0 release introduced human-curated community feeds as a social discovery mechanism, where users act as curators by reposting videos into thematic feeds that others can subscribe to \[48\]. This represents a manual form of social filtering. An algorithmic version could automate this by tracking which creators are frequently engaged with by accounts in the user’s social neighborhood. The risk is echo chambers: if users primarily follow people with similar interests, graph-based ranking reinforces existing preferences.

##### 3.2.3.2.6 Hybrid Approaches

Most production recommendation systems use hybrid approaches that combine multiple algorithm families. Netflix adopted a hybrid recommender combining collaborative and content-based filtering through its Netflix Prize competition \[43\]. For OpenReel, a practical hybrid would use content-based filtering as the default baseline (low data requirements, no opt-in needed) and layer in collaborative filtering signals as users choose to share richer engagement data. An exploration component can be added to deliberately surface some low-view-count or new-creator videos, counteracting popularity bias and giving emerging creators a discovery pathway. The Milli et al. (2025) PNAS Nexus study further suggests that hybrid approaches incorporating users’ stated preferences alongside behavioral signals can reduce hostile content amplification without sacrificing satisfaction, an insight that maps directly onto OpenReel’s philosophy of user control.

#### **4.2.3.3 Comparison of Algorithm Families**

| Method | Individual Data Required | Cold-Start Resilience | Transparency | Filter Bubble Risk |
| ----- | ----- | ----- | ----- | ----- |
| Chronological | None | Excellent | Perfect | None |
| Heuristic / Popularity | None (aggregates only) | Good | High (weights public) | Moderate |
| Collaborative Filtering | Shared engagement (opt-in) | Poor | Low-Moderate | Moderate |
| Content-Based | User's own history (local) | Moderate | High | High |
| Social / Graph-Based | Follow graph (public) | Moderate | High | Moderate |
| Hybrid | Varies by component | Good | Moderate | Manageable |

#### **4.2.3.4 Engagement Signals for Video Recommendation**

The effectiveness of any recommendation algorithm depends on the quality of input signals. OpenReel’s primary data-layer contribution is defining new Lexicon schemas that capture video-native engagement data currently absent from AT Protocol. The signals needed fall into four categories.

Explicit signals include likes, reposts, follows, bookmarks, and saves. These already exist as AT Protocol record types. They are high-confidence indicators of preference but are sparse and binary, a like conveys positive sentiment but not intensity.

Implicit behavioral signals are where OpenReel’s new Lexicon schemas provide the most value. Watch duration (both absolute and as a fraction of video length), completion rate, rewatch count, whether sound was enabled, scroll-past speed, and pause events all capture engagement intensity that explicit signals cannot. Completion rate is arguably the single most informative signal for short-form video: it distinguishes autoplay-triggered views from genuine engagement. TikTok’s own documentation identifies watch time as one of the strongest ranking factors, and its algorithm heavily weights the first few seconds of viewing behavior \[49\].

Content metadata includes tags, captions, audio track identifiers, creator ID, video duration, upload timestamp, and content safety classification labels. This data is attached to the video record itself and is available to all feed generators.

Social graph data encompasses the public follow graph and second-order connections. This is already available via AT Protocol’s firehose and requires no additional data collection.

A key architectural principle in OpenReel is that individual-level implicit signals are stored on the user’s PDS (they own it), while feed generators only see anonymized aggregates (average completion rate, total view count) by default. Users who want more personalized recommendations can opt in to sharing granular engagement data with specific feed generators. This privacy-tiered access model determines which algorithm families are available at each level of data sharing.

### **4.2.4 Application to OpenReel**

Given the research above, the recommended strategy for OpenReel is a tiered approach that maps algorithm families to the platform’s privacy-tiered data architecture.

#### **4.2.4.1 Default Feeds (No Individual Data Required)**

OpenReel should ship with three default feeds that require no individual user data opt-in. First, a Chronological feed showing recent posts from followed accounts, serving as the transparent baseline. Second, a Trending / Heuristic feed ranking content by a weighted combination of aggregate signals (completion rate, like ratio, recency), with the weights publicly displayed. Third, a Social Discovery feed surfacing videos from creators within the user’s extended follow graph. These three feeds cover the primary consumption modes, intentional following, popular discovery, and social discovery, without requiring any data beyond what AT Protocol already makes public.

#### **4.2.4.2 Opt-In Personalized Feeds**

When users opt in to sharing engagement data with specific feed generators, more sophisticated algorithms become available. A content-based personalized feed can recommend videos matching the user’s demonstrated interests by analyzing their engagement history against content metadata. A collaborative filtering feed can leverage cross-user behavioral patterns to surface genuinely novel content. A hybrid feed combining both approaches with an exploration component can balance personalization, novelty, and new-creator visibility.

#### **4.2.4.3 Third-Party Feed Generator API**

The AT Protocol’s feed generator architecture, as documented in Bluesky’s official starter kit and custom feed tutorial, provides the foundation for OpenReel’s pluggable algorithm system. Feed generators are HTTP servers that receive a getFeedSkeleton request and return a list of post URIs; the requesting server handles hydration \[42\]. This means any developer can build and deploy a custom recommendation algorithm without needing access to OpenReel’s infrastructure. Bluesky’s recent launch of Attie, an AI tool that translates natural language into custom feed algorithms, further validates the viability of democratized feed generation \[50\]. OpenReel’s contribution is to extend this model with video-specific engagement data via new Lexicon schemas, so that third-party feed generators can access richer signals than what text-based social feeds currently provide.

#### **4.2.4.4 Method Selection**

The selected approach is a layered multi-algorithm strategy rather than a single algorithm. This is the method best suited to OpenReel’s architecture for three reasons. First, it aligns with the project’s core value proposition: user choice over algorithmic curation. Forcing all users through a single recommendation pipeline, even a sophisticated one, would replicate the centralized design that OpenReel exists to challenge. Second, the tiered approach maps naturally onto the privacy-tiered data model already specified in OpenReel’s architecture, where different algorithms become available at different opt-in levels. Third, the research evidence from Milli et al. \[41\] demonstrates that engagement-optimized algorithms do not necessarily align with user satisfaction, making it important to offer alternatives with different objective functions.

## **4.3 Research Topic: Video Delivery and Playback Architecture**

### **4.3.1 Introduction**

Short form video platforms live or die based on how well they can get video to a user's screen fast and keep it playing without interruption. For OpenReel, which is a decentralized iOS app built on the AT Protocol, getting this right means making a series of connected architectural decisions: which streaming protocol to use, where video files are stored and how they get delivered, what codec the video is encoded in, which iOS framework handles playback, and how the app preloads and buffers video in a fast scrolling feed. Each of these decisions affects the others. A bad choice at the streaming protocol layer breaks the codec decision. A bad storage choice breaks delivery latency. This section works through each of those decisions in order, applying the research to OpenReel's specific constraints as an iOS-only, AT Protocol-based short form video platform.

### **4.3.2 Problem Statement**

OpenReel must design a complete video delivery and playback architecture that covers streaming protocol, storage and CDN, video encoding, iOS playback framework, and buffering and preloading strategy in a way that produces smooth, reliable, low-latency video playback in a fast scrolling full screen feed on iOS without buffering interruptions or audio bleed between clips.

### **4.3.3 Streaming Protocol**

#### **Research**

HLS, originally developed by Apple in 2009, operates by segmenting video content into small chunks and delivering them over standard HTTP. The client dynamically adjusts which quality tier to request based on available bandwidth, a technique known as Adaptive Bitrate Streaming (ABR). This means a user on 5G receives high resolution video while a user on LTE receives a lower resolution version of the same content, both without manual intervention. HLS segments are typically two to six seconds in length, and Apple introduced Low Latency HLS (LL-HLS) to push latency between two seconds for near real time delivery \[51\]. Critically for OpenReel, HLS is the only adaptive streaming protocol with native support on iOS and Safari. Apple devices cannot play MPEG-DASH without a custom software player, making HLS the de facto standard for any application targeting the Apple ecosystem \[52\].

MPEG-DASH, developed by the Moving Picture Experts Group as an international open standard, functions similarly to HLS in its segment based, ABR delivery model. Its primary distinction is codec agnosticism: DASH supports any codec, including AV1 and VP9, which can produce files 30 to 50 percent smaller than H.264 at equivalent quality \[53\]. DASH is the dominant protocol for cross-platform streaming, used by services like Netflix, YouTube, and Hulu. However, DASH has no native support on iOS or Safari. Streaming DASH to an iPhone requires an HTML5 player like Shaka Player or DASH.js, which introduces additional dependencies and defeats the performance advantages of native playback \[54\].

Progressive Download is the simplest delivery method  the server streams a single video file to the client, which begins playback as soon as enough data is buffered. There is no manifest file, no multiple quality tiers, and no dynamic bitrate switching. A 2026 peer reviewed study published in the Journal of the Institution of Engineers India confirmed that adaptive protocols like DASH and HLS significantly outperform progressive download in buffering frequency and error recovery under variable network conditions \[51\]. Progressive download serves a single fixed resolution to all devices regardless of screen size or connection quality, making it unsuitable for a mobile platform where network conditions vary widely. If a video was encoded at 1080p and a user's connection drops, the video simply buffers rather than gracefully downgrading quality \[55\]

| Feature | HLS | MPEG-DASH | Progressive Download |
| ----- | ----- | ----- | ----- |
| Adaptive Bitrate (ABR) | Yes | Yes | No |
| iOS / Safari Native Support | Yes (natively) | No (requires custom player) | Yes (basic MP4) |
| Codec Support | H.264, H.265 | Any (AV1, VP9, H.264, H.265) | Any single codec |
| Latency | Low with LL-HLS | Low with chunked transfer | High |
| Open Standard | No (Apple proprietary) | Yes (ISO/IEC 23009-1) | N/A |
| Implementation Complexity on iOS | Low (native AVPlayer) | Medium (requires 3rd party player) | Very Low |
| Network Resilience | High | High | Low |
| Used By | Apple, Twitch, Akamai | Netflix, YouTube, Hulu | Legacy / simple web players |

#### **Application to OpenReel**

OpenReel's project documentation explicitly identifies buffering interruptions and audio bleed between videos as known pain points in existing AT Protocol video applications, symptoms directly tied to inadequate streaming protocol implementation. OpenReel targets iPhone 12 and later running iOS 16 or higher, an entirely Apple hardware environment. This context makes the streaming protocol decision straightforward: HLS is natively supported by AVPlayer, Apple's built in media playback framework, and requires no third party player library. MPEG-DASH would require integrating an additional Swift or JavaScript based player, adding complexity and potential performance overhead to an app already managing decentralized identity, custom feed generators, and content classification. Progressive download is eliminated given that OpenReel's non-functional requirements mandate graceful handling of network interruptions, a behavior progressive download cannot support.

The fast scrolling feed use case reinforces this decision further. When a user swipes to the next video, the app needs to preload the upcoming clip while tearing down the current one. HLS's segmented architecture makes this straightforward: the app can begin fetching the first few segments of the next video in the feed before the user reaches it. This is precisely the behavior needed to eliminate the buffering interruptions that the project documentation identifies as a problem in existing AT Protocol video apps.

#### **Selected Method**

HLS is the selected streaming protocol for OpenReel. Its native integration with iOS and AVPlayer eliminates third party dependencies, its adaptive bitrate capabilities directly address the buffering and playback reliability requirements in the project documentation, and its segmented architecture supports the preloading strategy required for smooth fast-scroll feed behavior. While MPEG-DASH offers broader codec flexibility and open standardization, those advantages are irrelevant for a platform exclusively targeting Apple devices. Progressive download is disqualified by its inability to adapt to variable network conditions. HLS delivers the most reliable, lowest overhead path to smooth video playback on iOS.

### **4.3.4 Video Storage and CDN Architecture**

#### **4.3.4.1 Research**

The AT Protocol's official specification explicitly says it is not a recommended or required pattern to serve video directly from the PDS to end users, and that servers do not need to support this use case (AT Protocol, "Blobs"). Instead the spec describes blobs as being authoritatively stored on the user's PDS but served to end users via CDNs associated with individual applications \[56\]. The AT Protocol community wiki backs this up, noting that blobs on a PDS are typically served via specialized protocol independent content delivery networks, and that this separation lets apps optimize delivery through transcoding and thumbnail generation \[57\]. This means using a CDN for video delivery is not just a performance decision for OpenReel, it is the architecturally intended pattern of the protocol itself.

Object storage plus a CDN is the standard architecture for video delivery. The pattern is to store video files in cloud object storage like Amazon S3, which acts as the origin, and put a CDN like Amazon CloudFront in front of it to cache video segments at edge locations near users \[58\]. Adding a CDN in front of an S3 bucket can drop video load time from over 25 seconds to under 6 seconds for a cached asset, and combining it with proper video optimization can get load time under 2 seconds \[59\]. CloudFront integrates with S3 through Origin Access Control so the bucket stays private and content only comes through the CDN \[58\].

IPFS is a decentralized peer-to-peer storage protocol that uses content addressing where files are identified by a hash rather than a server location. This lines up philosophically with AT Protocol's use of CIDs for blobs. But IPFS has real problems for a short form video platform. Retrieval speed depends on which peers are currently hosting a file, with no guarantee of low latency unless pinning services are used. For a fast scroll feed where a clip needs to start playing almost immediately after a swipe, the unpredictability of peer-to-peer retrieval is a dealbreaker \[60\].

Direct PDS serving fails at scale. The AT Protocol's own GitHub discussion on PDS implementation confirms blobs are stored per account on the PDS but that CDN delivery is the recommended pattern \[61\]. A PDS is designed to be lightweight and handle identity and repository operations, not thousands of concurrent video stream requests.

| Feature | PDS Direct Serving | S3 \+ CDN (AWS) | IPFS |
| ----- | ----- | ----- | ----- |
| Latency | High (no edge caching) | Low (edge cached globally) | Variable (peer-dependent) |
| Scalability | Poor | Excellent | Unpredictable |
| AT Protocol Compatibility | Technically possible, not recommended | Architecturally intended pattern | Philosophically aligned, not practical |
| Reliability | Depends on PDS operator | 99.9%+ AWS SLA | Depends on peer availability |
| Decentralization | High | Moderate | High |

#### **Application to OpenReel**

OpenReel's hardware requirements already confirm AWS S3 as the media storage layer and a CDN as required infrastructure. This lines up with the AT Protocol's own guidance: blobs live on the PDS as the authoritative source and delivery to users goes through a CDN. In OpenReel's case, when a user uploads a video, the file is stored on their PDS as a blob referenced by a CID. The AppView layer then serves it to other users through CloudFront, which caches HLS segments at edge locations. The PDS stays the authoritative owner of the content, keeping user data portability and decentralized ownership intact, while CloudFront handles the performance-sensitive delivery the fast scroll feed needs.

#### **Selected Method**

AWS S3 combined with Amazon CloudFront is the selected storage and delivery architecture. Video blobs are stored on the user's PDS as the portable, cryptographically signed source of truth. The AppView serves video to users via CloudFront. This follows the AT Protocol's own recommended separation of blob storage from media delivery, matches the confirmed hardware requirements, and is a well proven architecture used by the largest video platforms in the world.

### **4.3.5 Video Encoding and Codec Selection**

#### **Research**

A codec is what compresses video files so they can be moved across the internet without destroying quality or overwhelming bandwidth. The three codecs relevant to OpenReel are H.264, H.265, and AV1. Choosing the right one affects file size, encoding cost, playback battery drain, and compatibility with the iPhone hardware targets.

H.264, also called AVC, has been the dominant video codec since 2003\. It works on almost every device ever made and has near universal support across iOS, browsers, and streaming stacks \[62\]. It encodes fast, hardware acceleration is everywhere, and it is the safest compatibility choice. The tradeoff is efficiency: at higher resolutions and lower bitrates, H.264 produces noticeably larger files than newer codecs.

H.265, also called HEVC, roughly doubles the compression efficiency of H.264, producing files about 40 to 50 percent smaller at the same visual quality \[63\]. iPhones have recorded video in H.265 by default since the iPhone 7, and hardware decode support is widespread across Apple devices. The downside is licensing: H.265 has four separate patent licensing pools which can create legal overhead for commercial platforms \[64\]. Netflix, Amazon Prime, and Apple TV+ use H.265 for 4K content.

AV1 is a royalty free open source codec developed by the Alliance for Open Media, a group that includes Google, Apple, Netflix, and Amazon. It compresses 30 to 50 percent better than H.264 and 20 to 30 percent better than H.265 at equivalent quality \[65\]. Netflix reported in December 2025 that AV1 sessions use one third less bandwidth than H.264 and H.265 and have 45 percent fewer buffering interruptions \[66\]. The problem is encoding speed: AV1 software encoding is still 5 to 10 times slower than H.265 \[66\]. Hardware AV1 decode support on Apple devices arrived with the A17 Pro chip in iPhone 15 Pro, meaning older iPhone targets like iPhone 12 may not decode AV1 efficiently \[67\].

| Feature | H.264 | H.265 (HEVC) | AV1 |
| ----- | ----- | ----- | ----- |
| Compression vs H.264 | Baseline | \~50% smaller | \~50% smaller |
| iOS Hardware Support | Universal | iPhone 7+ | iPhone 15 Pro+ (A17 Pro) |
| Encoding Speed | Fast | Medium | 5–10x slower than H.265 |
| Licensing | Royalty-bearing | Royalty-bearing (4 pools) | Royalty-free |
| Battery Drain on Decode | Low | Low–Medium | Higher on older devices |
| OpenReel Risk | None | Low (some licensing overhead) | High (iPhone 12 decode concern) |

#### **Application to OpenReel**

OpenReel targets iPhone 12 and later. AV1 hardware decode support on Apple devices only fully arrived with the A17 Pro in iPhone 15 Pro, meaning an iPhone 12 or 13 user would decode AV1 in software, draining battery and risking choppy playback. That rules out AV1 as a primary codec for OpenReel's current hardware targets. H.265 is the stronger choice over H.264 given that iPhones already record in H.265 natively, meaning uploaded videos arrive in H.265 with no re-encoding required in the common case. The 40 to 50 percent file size reduction over H.264 also directly lowers CDN egress costs. H.264 should be kept as a fallback for any edge cases where H.265 playback fails.

#### **Selected Method**

H.265 is the selected primary codec for OpenReel, with H.264 as a fallback. H.265 reduces bandwidth and storage costs, is already the native recording format for iPhones in OpenReel's target range, and has full hardware decode support across the iPhone 12 and later lineup. AV1 is promising for future consideration as Apple expands hardware decode support, but it is not viable as a primary codec for OpenReel's current target devices.

### **4.3.6 iOS Playback Framework**

#### **Research**

Once the streaming protocol, storage architecture, and codec are decided, the question becomes: what plays the video on the device? On iOS there are two primary options, Apple's native AVFoundation framework and third party player libraries.

AVFoundation is Apple's full featured framework for time based audiovisual media on iOS, iPadOS, macOS, tvOS, visionOS, and watchOS \[68\]. The core playback class is AVPlayer, which manages playback of a single asset. For sequential playback across multiple videos, AVQueuePlayer is a subclass of AVPlayer that accepts an array of AVPlayerItems and plays them in order \[69\]. For display, AVPlayerLayer renders the video into a Core Animation layer which maps naturally to a UIKit or SwiftUI view. AVFoundation natively supports HLS, handles ABR variant switching, manages audio session routing to prevent audio bleed, and integrates directly with iOS system features like AirPlay and Picture in Picture. In iOS 18, Apple introduced new AVMetrics APIs that expose detailed HLS performance events including startup time, stall events, and variant switch events, giving developers fine grained visibility into playback health \[70\].

Third party player libraries like VLCKit, IJKPlayer, and VideoKit are built on top of FFmpeg or other open source decoders and provide additional codec support beyond what AVFoundation natively handles. They are commonly used when an app needs to play formats that AVFoundation does not support. For a platform using HLS and H.265 on iOS, neither of those conditions applies. Third party players add binary size, introduce maintenance overhead, and sit outside of Apple's optimization path for iOS hardware video decode acceleration \[55\].

| Feature | AVFoundation / AVPlayer | Third Party (VLCKit, IJKPlayer) |
| ----- | ----- | ----- |
| HLS Support | Native | Yes (via FFmpeg) |
| H.265 Hardware Decode | Yes (native) | Varies |
| Audio Session Management | Built-in | Manual |
| System Integration (AirPlay, PiP) | Built-in | Manual or unavailable |
| iOS 18 Performance Metrics | Yes (AVMetrics) | No |
| Binary Size Impact | None (system framework) | Adds 20–60 MB |
| AVQueuePlayer for Feed | Yes (native sequential playback) | No native equivalent |

#### **Application to OpenReel**

OpenReel's entire stack is iOS native and uses HLS with H.265. There is no reason to introduce a third party player. AVFoundation handles everything needed: HLS playback, H.265 hardware decode, audio session management to prevent the audio bleed the project documentation specifically calls out, and AVQueuePlayer for managing sequential video playback in the feed. The iOS 18 AVMetrics APIs are directly useful for monitoring playback stalls and variant switch events in production, which maps to the non-functional requirement for graceful handling of network interruptions.

#### **Selected Method**

AVFoundation with AVPlayer and AVQueuePlayer is the selected iOS playback framework. It is the native, zero-dependency solution that covers every playback requirement for OpenReel's HLS and H.265 stack, integrates with iOS system features out of the box, and gives the team production-grade metrics through the iOS 18 AVMetrics APIs.

### **4.3.7 Buffering, Preloading, and Fast-Scroll Playback**

#### **Research**

The previous four sections establish the full pipeline from storage to playback: HLS over S3 and CloudFront, encoded in H.265, played through AVFoundation. The final question is how the app manages that pipeline under the conditions specific to a TikTok style vertical feed: rapid sequential video transitions triggered by user swipes, where a clip needs to be ready to play almost instantly.

Mux published a detailed guide on building smooth scrolling iOS video feeds, noting that users today expect videos to load fast and if a video stalls or buffers they will quickly keep scrolling or close the app \[71\]. The standard approach is preloading: beginning to fetch and buffer the next video in the feed before the user swipes to it. For HLS specifically, preloading means initializing an AVPlayerItem with the manifest URL before the video becomes visible, which triggers the HLS manifest fetch and the first segment download without starting audio or visual playback \[72\]. This puts the video in a ready to play state so that when the user swipes, playback starts immediately.

Memory management is the other side of preloading. Naively preloading ten videos ahead of the user and never releasing them behind will eventually crash the app. The practical pattern used in production short form video feeds is directional preloading: preloading a few videos ahead in the scroll direction and aggressively releasing or nulling out videos that have already been watched and are far behind the current position \[72\]. TikTok's own approach to this problem involves media player pooling, where a set of pre configured player instances are maintained and reused rather than initializing a new player for every video, reducing startup latency \[73\].

Audio bleed is the specific failure mode where the audio from a previous video continues playing while the next video has started, or where multiple videos play audio simultaneously. This is a known problem in AT Protocol video apps noted in OpenReel's project documentation. The fix is correct AVAudioSession management: when a video leaves the viewport, AVPlayer should be explicitly paused and the audio session should be deactivated before the next video's player activates. AVFoundation handles the audio session infrastructure, but the app is responsible for calling pause on the outgoing player at the right time \[55\].

| Strategy | No Preloading | Aggressive Preloading (All) | Directional Preloading (2–3 ahead) |
| ----- | ----- | ----- | ----- |
| Time to First Frame | Slow (fetches on demand) | Fast | Fast |
| Memory Usage | Low | Very High (crash risk) | Controlled |
| Bandwidth Usage | Low | Very High | Moderate |
| Audio Bleed Risk | Low (one player active) | High (multiple players initialized) | Low (with proper pause logic) |
| Scroll Responsiveness | Poor | Good | Good |
| Recommended For | Low traffic apps | Not recommended | Short-form video feeds |

#### **Application to OpenReel**

OpenReel's feed should implement directional preloading: initializing AVPlayerItems for the next two to three videos in the feed as soon as the current video starts playing, triggering HLS manifest and first segment fetches in the background without starting audio. When a user swipes, the preloaded item is already in memory and ready to play. Videos behind the current position should have their AVPlayerItem replaced with nil to release memory. Audio bleed is prevented by explicitly pausing the outgoing AVPlayer and deactivating its audio session before activating the incoming one. This directly addresses the audio bleed problem called out in OpenReel's project documentation.

#### **Selected Method**

Directional preloading with AVQueuePlayer, explicit audio session management, and aggressive memory release behind the current scroll position is the selected buffering and preloading strategy. This approach keeps time to first frame low, prevents audio bleed, and manages memory within acceptable limits for the iPhone 12 and later hardware targets. It is the same pattern used in production by short form video platforms at scale.

### **4.3.8 Section Summary: Video Delivery Architecture Decisions**

Video delivery and playback architecture for OpenReel comes down to five connected decisions that each constrain the next. HLS is the only viable streaming protocol for an iOS only platform. S3 and CloudFront are the storage and delivery layer recommended by the AT Protocol spec itself. H.265 is the right codec given that iPhones already record in it and it cuts bandwidth costs versus H.264 with full hardware support across OpenReel's target devices. AVFoundation is the native, zero dependency playback framework that handles HLS, H.265, audio session management, and sequential feed playback out of the box. And directional preloading with proper audio session teardown is what turns those four choices into a smooth, buffer free, audio bleed free feed experience. Together these decisions address all of the playback pain points identified in OpenReel's project documentation: buffering interruptions, audio bleed, and inconsistent playback state during fast scrolling.

| Decision Area | Selected Approach | Key Rationale |
| ----- | ----- | ----- |
| Streaming Protocol | HLS | Native iOS/AVPlayer support; adaptive bitrate; no third-party dependencies |
| Storage and CDN | AWS S3 \+ Amazon CloudFront | AT Protocol's own recommended blob delivery pattern; proven global scale |
| Video Codec | H.265 (HEVC), H.264 fallback | Native iPhone recording format; 40–50% smaller than H.264; full hardware decode on iPhone 12+ |
| iOS Playback Framework | AVFoundation (AVPlayer \+ AVQueuePlayer) | Native, zero-dependency; handles HLS, H.265, audio session, AirPlay, PiP, iOS 18 AVMetrics |
| Buffering Strategy | Directional preloading (2–3 ahead) \+ audio session teardown | Instant swipe responsiveness; prevents audio bleed and memory crashes |

## **4.4 Research Topic: Federated Service Architecture for Short-Form Video**

### **4.4.1 Introduction**

One of the most important novelties in OpenReel will be its unique architecture to enable a federated, collaborative system for delivering short-form video content in a decentralized and non-monopolized manner. Whereas traditional players in the social media space follow a standard client-server architecture and own the components from end to end, federation introduces an interesting system design challenge that requires careful research and planning. To this end, we analyze the architectures of similar platforms in the social media space: Mastodon, a federated social media platform where communities have separate servers and websites, and Bluesky, a decentralized Twitter-like platform designed on the AT Protocol \[74\], \[76\]. Finally, we consider possible architectural design choices unique to video, decentralization, and modularity.

### **4.4.2 Problem Statement**

The OpenReel team must determine how to architect a federated short-form video platform in which identity, metadata, media storage, and recommendation are handled by interoperable services that preserve user ownership and portability while still delivering scalable discovery, low-latency playback, and manageable system complexity.

### **4.4.3 Analysis of Similar Platforms**

The focus of this section will be the introduction and comparison of the architectures of Mastodon and Bluesky.

#### **4.4.3.1 Mastodon System Design**

Mastodon is a decentralized, open-source social networking platform built on the ActivityPub protocol. It operates as a federation of independent servers (called “instances”), each run by different administrators \[74\]. These instances communicate with each other over the ActivityPub protocol, forming a larger network termed the “Fediverse” (federated universe). A user on one instance may follow, reply to, and interact with users on any other federated instance.

Mastodon is a self-contained Rails application composed of \[75\]:

- A Ruby on Rails backend that handles the web application logic, REST API, and ActivityPub federation. It exposes a full API that the web and mobile frontends consume.  
- A PostgreSQL database server, which stores users, posts, relationships, and metadata.
