- Redis for caching, job queues, and timeline management.  
- A Sidekiq (background job worker) instance that handles asynchronous tasks like delivering posts to remote instances, processing incoming federation messages, sending emails, and media processing.  
- A Node.js WebSocket streaming server, pushing real-time updates, such as new posts and notifications, to connected clients.

Images, videos, and other media are stored using object storage services, such as Amazon S3.

![][image1]  
*Figure: Mastodon software architecture diagram \[75\].*

#### **4.4.3.2 Bluesky System Design**

Bluesky is a decentralized social networking platform built on the AT Protocol, which it developed as an open standard (Kleppmann). It is inspired by the architecture of the web itself. User repositories are hosted on personal data servers and content is “indexed” as with web search engines.

The AT Protocol separates concerns into three main services \[76\]:

- The Personal Data Server (PDS): A PDS acts as the user’s agent in the network by hosting their data (such as their posts), handling authentication and account management, controlling signing keys, storing any private data, and acting as the API gateway. Users may self-host a PDS or use a managed provider.  
- The Relay: The Relay crawls the entire network to gather data and outputs it into a stream (the “firehose”) for other services to consume.  
- App View: This service indexes the firehose data and serves the actual application API. AppViews can implement invite systems, custom algorithms, alternative clients, varying monetization and content moderation strategies, and off-protocol services. Bluesky is the largest App View.

![][image2]  
*Figure: Bluesky software architecture diagram (“Federation Architecture”).*

There are additional service layers, including custom feed generators and independent moderation services that label content as “NSFW”, “misinformation”, etc. Users and App Views can subscribe to trusted labelers, decoupling moderation from hosting.

#### **4.4.3.3 Comparison of Mastodon and Bluesky Architectures**

Mastodon and Bluesky both pursue decentralization, but they differ fundamentally in how they achieve it. Mastodon follows a P2P federation model: each instance is a fully self-contained application that communicates directly with other instances over ActivityPub \[75\], \[74\]. Hence, there is no central aggregation point, and each server discovers and stores only content it encounters through its users’ follows and interactions. Two instances may have different views of the same conversation, since a server only knows about replies that have been delivered to it.

Bluesky, by contrast, separates the network into specialized layers. A user’s PDS is lightweight and only manages their data and authentication, offloading the need to index and serve content to other components (“Federation Architecture”). Specifically, this responsibility is handled by the Relay, which crawls all PDSes and produces a unified firehose, and by the App View, which indexes the firehose and serves the application API. This layered approach gives every user and App View a consistent, global view of the network, at the cost of making the Relay an expensive and operationally demanding component.

| Dimension | Mastodon | Bluesky |
| ----- | ----- | ----- |
| Protocol | ActivityPub (W3C standard) | AT Protocol |
| Federation Model | P2P; instances communicate directly | Layered; PDS, Relay, and AppView as separate services |
| Content Consistency | Partial; each instance only sees content delivered to it | Global; the Relay aggregates all network activity |
| Identity | Tied to instance; lost if instance shuts down | Decentralized IDs (DIDs) independent of any server |
| Data Portability | Limited; follower migration supported, post history is not | Full; cryptographically signed data can move between PDSs |
| Moderation | Instance-level; administrators set and enforce local policies | Decoupled; independent labelers annotate content, users choose which to trust |
| Scalability Concerns | Large instances bear disproportionate federation traffic | Relay must process entire network output |
| Server Complexity | High; each instance runs the full application stack | Low per component; higher overall coordination complexity |

A key difference applicable to OpenReel's architecture is in identity management and data portability. In Mastodon, a user's identity is tied to their instance. If their instance shuts down, the account and content is permanently lost unless the administrator performs a migration. In Bluesky, identity is anchored to cryptographic decentralized identifiers (DIDs) that are independent of any server. A user's data lives in a signed repository that can be moved to a different PDS without losing posts, follows, or likes. From a scalability perspective, the two approaches also face different bottlenecks. Mastodon instances scale independently, but large instances bear disproportionate federation traffic since they must deliver and receive posts to and from many remote servers. This is similar to the early days of Facebook, where each college campus had a dedicated server that was independent from other Facebook servers. Bluesky's Relay must process the entire network's output, making it resource-intensive, but individual PDSes remain lightweight since they only need to manage their own users' data.

### **4.4.4 Architectural Considerations for Short-Form Video**

For OpenReel, both described architectures present instructive tradeoffs. Mastodon’s model distributes storage and compute across many independent servers, which aligns well with the high storage demands of video but introduces inconsistency in content availability. Bluesky’s model offers a consistent global view and cleaner separation of concerns, but its reliance on a centralized Relay and App View layer raises questions about how well that pattern scales when the content being indexed and served is video rather than text (Kleppmann). This implies that Bluesky’s bottleneck, the Relay, could become even more of a bottleneck in a similar video-centric platform.

However, this bottleneck can be mitigated by recognizing that the Relay does not need to handle video content directly. In Bluesky’s architecture, the Relay aggregates lightweight data records, like posts, likes, and follows, rather than the media itself. A video platform can follow the same principle: the Relay and firehose carry only metadata (references to videos, captions, tags, engagement signals), while the actual video files are stored and served separately through PDSs. This logical separation means that the Relay’s scaling laws are maintained regardless of whether the underlying content is a short text post or a 60-second video. The expensive operations unique to video, including transcoding, thumbnail generation, and delivery, can be pushed to the storage and distribution layer, where they are handled by the user’s PDS provider.

### **4.4.5 Proposed Architecture for OpenReel**

OpenReel should hence be built on the AT Protocol, adopting its layered separation of concerns as the foundation for a federated short-form video platform. Users will authenticate through DIDs, ensuring that identity and data are portable across providers. Video content and metadata will be stored in PDS, making them portable and self-authenticating in the same manner as Bluesky. The recommendation system will be designed as a pluggable, open interface taken from Bluesky’s custom feed generators. While we generally follow Bluesky’s approach, we recognize the value of Mastodon’s perspective, which can be used to guide the storage and processing of video content, where PDSs can be viewed in a similar lens to Mastodon instances. The full system design will be covered in a future section

### **4.4.6 Conclusion**

Mastodon and Bluesky demonstrate two viable but distinct approaches to decentralized social networking. Mastodon's peer-to-peer federation distributes responsibility across independent instances but introduces inconsistency and ties identity to individual servers. Bluesky's layered architecture offers global consistency and strong data portability at the cost of concentrating certain infrastructure roles. By building on the AT Protocol and separating video delivery from metadata aggregation, OpenReel can combine the strengths of both approaches, resulting in a consistent, portable, and modular foundation that remains scalable under the unique demands of short-form video content.

## **4.5 Research Topic: Content Moderation in Decentralized Video**

### **4.5.1 Introduction**

One of the most consequential design challenges in building OpenReel is determining how to handle content moderation in a federated environment. Centralized platforms such as YouTube and TikTok benefit from unified policy enforcement, large moderation teams, and proprietary automated tools that can be applied consistently across all user-generated content. A decentralized platform, by contrast, distributes content hosting and identity across many independent servers, making it impossible to rely on any single actor to police the entire network. At the same time, a platform without meaningful moderation is just as likely to become a venue for harmful content as any centralized alternative \[77\]. To this end, we examine how existing federated platforms, Mastodon and Bluesky, approach moderation, compare their methods, and consider what automated classification and labeling techniques are available to supplement human oversight. Finally, we propose a moderation strategy appropriate for OpenReel.

### **4.5.2 Problem Statement**

The team must choose a content moderation approach that can keep OpenReel's feeds safe and trustworthy without relying on a single centralized authority, while remaining practical for independent server operators and scalable to a growing network of short-form video content.

### **4.5.3 Analysis of Moderation Models**

The following section examines how Mastodon and Bluesky each approach content moderation in a decentralized context. 

#### **4.5.3.1 Mastodon: Instance-Level Community Moderation**

Mastodon uses community-based moderation, in which each server can limit or filter out undesirable types of content. The founder of Mastodon, Eugen Rochko, believes that small, closely related communities deal with unwanted behavior more effectively than a large company's small safety team \[78\]. In practice, this means that each instance administrator sets and enforces their own community guidelines, and moderation is carried out by a combination of paid staff and volunteers at the instance level.

Mastodon provides instance administrators with a tiered set of moderation tools. At the account level, administrators can warn, limit, or suspend individual users. A Mastodon suspension means the account is effectively deleted, the account no longer appears in search, the profile page is gone, and all posts, uploads, followers, and other data are removed publicly \[79\]. At the server level, administrators can apply domain blocks of varying severity, ranging from rejecting media files to fully suspending a remote server such that no content is stored locally except usernames \[79\].

When an instance's moderation is inadequate, other instances may choose to cut ties with it entirely, a practice known as defederation. In less extreme cases, admins may silence an instance instead of blocking it outright, allowing individual users to still follow accounts from the silenced server but preventing those users from boosting that content to others on their home instance \[80\]. Shared blocklists, distributed via community channels such as the \#fediblock hashtag, allow administrators to coordinate defederation decisions across multiple instances. Several prominent shared blocklists have emerged across the Fediverse, accompanied by tools that support their integration and management, enabling a shift from individual to collective approaches in content moderation \[81\].

The principal limitation of this model is its inconsistency. Because each instance sets its own rules, the same content may be allowed on one server and banned on another, leading to a fragmented and unpredictable experience across the network. Additionally, moderation responses are typically not proactive but reactive, users report problematic content they see on the federated timeline, and only then do admins and moderators consider defederation or silencing the offending instance \[80\].

#### **4.5.3.2 Bluesky: Open Protocol-Level Labeling**

Bluesky takes a fundamentally different approach by decoupling moderation from content hosting entirely. Rather than delegating moderation responsibility to instance administrators, Bluesky introduces an open, protocol-level labeling system in which any individual or organization can operate an independent moderation service.

Labeling is a key part of moderation, it is a system for marking content that may need to be hidden, blurred, taken down, or annotated in applications. By building an open-source labeling system, Bluesky's goal is to empower developers, organizations, and users to actively participate in shaping the future of moderation \[84\]. Independent labeling services, called labelers, publish annotations on content across the network. Client applications then read those labels to decide what to hide, blur, or drop, and since clients choose their own labelers and how to interpret the labels, each application can decide which moderation systems to support \[84\].

To lower the barrier for running a labeling service, Bluesky open-sourced its internal moderation tool, Ozone. With Ozone, individuals and teams can work together to review and label content across the network \[82\]. The AT Protocol implements a multi-layered approach to content moderation that combines platform-level controls, service-based labeling, and user preferences to create a flexible and decentralized moderation system \[83\]. This architecture is described as stackable: the Bluesky application hardcodes its own labeling and then stacks community labelers on top, so users can subscribe to additional moderation services on top of the existing foundation \[84\].

#### **4.5.3.3 YouTube / Centralized Moderation: Hybrid AI \+ Human Review**

YouTube represents the third major moderation paradigm: large-scale centralized enforcement combining proprietary AI classifiers with a global human review workforce. Nearly 98% of videos removed from YouTube for violent extremism are flagged by machine learning algorithms before any human reviews them \[85\]. YouTube's Content ID system uses hash-matching to detect known copyrighted material and illegal content across hundreds of millions of videos. Borderline content (content that does not violate policies but is deemed low quality or harmful) is deprioritized in recommendations without removal, an approach YouTube calls reducing \[86\].

The limitations of centralized enforcement are significant: it requires enormous resources to maintain at scale, creates a single point of both failure and abuse (platform can suppress content for non-policy reasons), and lacks transparency. Creators have reported opaque demonetization decisions and no meaningful appeals process. However, the advantages are real: consistent enforcement network-wide, fast response to emerging harmful content types, and no dependence on end-user configuration.

### **4.5.4 Comparison of Moderation Models**

| Dimension | Mastodon (Instance-Level) | Bluesky (Open Labeling) | YouTube (Centralized AI) |
| ----- | ----- | ----- | ----- |
| Moderation Authority | Instance administrators | Independent labelers chosen by users | Platform (YouTube/Google) |
| Policy Scope | Local; varies per instance | Network-wide; consistent per labeler | Global; uniform enforcement |
| User Control | Limited; set by instance admin | High; users subscribe to labelers | None; platform decides |
| Consistency | Inconsistent across instances | Consistent per labeler | Highly consistent |
| Enforcement Tools | Silencing, suspension, defederation | Content labels (hide, blur, takedown) | AI flagging, demonetization, removal, appeals |
| Automated Moderation | Minimal; primarily human-driven | Optional per labeler | Extensive; AI-first at scale |
| Transparency | High (instance policies are public) | High (labeler criteria are public) | Low (proprietary classifiers) |
| Risk | Harmful instances may go unblocked | Users may opt out of needed moderation | Over-removal; opaque enforcement; platform abuse |

A key distinction that is especially relevant to OpenReel is how each model handles cross-network harmful content. In Mastodon, a piece of harmful content hosted on a poorly moderated instance will only be blocked by those instances that have chosen to defederate from it. A user on an instance that has not yet defederated may still encounter it. In Bluesky, a labeler can annotate that content network-wide, and any user subscribed to that labeler will immediately see the content hidden or blurred, regardless of which PDS hosts it.

### **4.5.5 Automated Content Moderation Techniques for Short-Form Video**

Human moderation alone is insufficient at scale for a video platform. Short-form video introduces moderation challenges that text-based platforms do not face: harmful content may be embedded in audio, visual imagery, or on-screen text, requiring multimodal analysis pipelines. Several automated techniques are relevant to OpenReel's design.

Hash-matching and perceptual hashing are effective for detecting known illegal or harmful content. Platforms like YouTube use a system called Content ID for hash-matching audio and video content in the copyright domain, and nearly 98% of videos removed from YouTube for violent extremism are flagged by machine learning algorithms \[85\]. Perceptual hashing can identify near-duplicate videos that have been slightly re-encoded or cropped, making it harder for bad actors to evade detection by making minor modifications.

Machine learning classifiers can flag harmful content before it is reviewed by a human moderator. Automated hash-matching and predictive machine learning tools are increasingly being deployed to conduct content moderation at scale by major platforms. Modern classification tools typically involve supervised learning, in which models are trained to predict outcomes based on labeled instances \[87\]. For video specifically, classifiers can be applied to individual frames, audio transcripts, and on-screen text simultaneously, with results aggregated to produce an overall risk score for a given clip.

Federated learning offers a promising approach for training moderation models without centralizing sensitive data. FedMod, a collaborative content moderation system based on federated learning, enables servers to exchange parameters of partially trained local content moderation models with similar servers, creating a shared federated model. Compared to purely local models, FedMod improves harmful content detection by 12.69%, bot content detection by 5.79%, and content warning assignment by 9.43% \[81\]. This approach is particularly well-suited to a decentralized platform like OpenReel, where no single operator controls enough data to train a high-quality classifier on their own.

Hybrid moderation pipelines, combining automated classification with human review, represent the practical state of the art. Hybrid moderation combines AI automation with human oversight, AI handles routine filtering of non-compliant content and escalates ambiguous or complex cases to human moderators \[88\]. For a decentralized video platform, this suggests that automated classifiers should serve as a first-pass filter that surfaces content for labeler review rather than making final removal decisions autonomously.

| Technique | Description |
| ----- | ----- |
| **Hash-matching / Perceptual hashing** | Effective for detecting known illegal or harmful content. Perceptual hashing can identify near-duplicate videos that have been slightly re-encoded or cropped, making it harder for bad actors to evade detection by making minor modifications. |
| **ML classifiers (frame \+ audio level)** | Can flag harmful content before human review. Classifiers can be applied to individual frames, audio transcripts, and on-screen text simultaneously, with results aggregated to produce an overall risk score. |
| **Federated learning (FedMod)** | Enables servers to exchange parameters of partially trained local content moderation models without pooling raw video data. FedMod improves harmful content detection by 12.69%, bot content detection by 5.79%, and content warning assignment by 9.43% over purely local models \[81\]. |
| **Hybrid pipeline (AI \+ human review)** | Combines automated classification with human oversight. AI handles routine filtering and escalates ambiguous cases to human moderators. This represents the practical state of the art for video platforms at scale. |

### **4.5.6 Proposed Moderation Strategy for OpenReel**

OpenReel adopts Bluesky's labeling architecture as its moderation foundation, extended with automated classification capabilities suited to video content, and informed by YouTube's hybrid AI \+ human review model for scale. The system operates on three stackable layers:

* Layer 1: Baseline Platform Moderation: OpenReel hardcodes a default labeling service enforcing minimum community standards network-wide. This covers clearly illegal content (CSAM, content promoting real-world violence) using perceptual hash-matching against known hash databases as an automated first pass.

* Layer 2: Open Labeler Ecosystem: Following Bluesky's model, OpenReel supports independent labeling services that any organization, community, or developer can operate. Labelers annotate video content with standardized labels (NSFW, graphic violence, misinformation) that client applications interpret and surface to users.

* Layer 3: Automated Classification Pipeline: OpenReel provides a reference implementation of a video classification pipeline that PDS operators and labelers can optionally deploy. This pipeline applies frame-level and audio-level classifiers to uploaded videos, generating candidate labels that human moderators review and confirm via an Ozone-compatible interface. Over time, PDS operators may share model parameters using a federated learning approach to improve classification quality without pooling raw video data.

This layered architecture combines the consistency of a global labeling system (borrowed from Bluesky), the scale efficiencies of automated AI classification (borrowed from YouTube), and the community-driven flexibility of open labelers, while avoiding the centralized control and opacity that makes YouTube's model a poor fit for a decentralized platform.

### **4.5.7 Conclusion**

Content moderation in a decentralized video platform cannot rely on any single actor or mechanism. Mastodon's instance-level approach distributes responsibility but produces inconsistent and reactive enforcement. Bluesky's open labeling system offers global reach and user agency, but requires complementary automated tools to be practical at the scale of a video platform. By combining Bluesky's stackable labeling architecture with automated classification pipelines informed by federated learning, OpenReel can achieve moderation that is consistent, transparent, and scalable, without sacrificing the decentralized principles that motivate its design.

## **4.6 Research Topic: Metadata Management with AT Protocol**

### **4.6.1 Introduction**

Metadata is what allows content on a social platform to be identified, described, and processed by different services. User identities, timestamps, captions, tags, media references, and interaction records are all forms of metadata. On centralized platforms, this information is usually stored in private databases and controlled entirely by one company. AT Protocol takes a different approach by making metadata portable, schema-defined, and interoperable across services. For OpenReel, this is important because a decentralized short-form video platform depends on structured metadata not only for storing video posts, but also for moderation, recommendation, and discovery \[76\], \[91\].

### **4.6.2 Problem Statement**

AT Protocol provides a strong metadata foundation through DIDs, repositories, and Lexicon schemas, but its default record types were designed mainly for text-first social media. OpenReel must determine how to represent video-specific metadata in a way that remains portable across providers, understandable to other services, and useful for downstream systems such as recommendation engines, moderation tools, and playback infrastructure \[89\], \[90\].

### **4.6.3 Research**

AT Protocol manages metadata across several layers. At the identity layer, users are represented by DIDs and DID documents, which describe account ownership, public keys, and where a user’s data is hosted \[89\]. At the repository layer, content is stored in signed personal repositories that preserve integrity and support portability between providers. At the schema layer, Lexicon defines the structure of records and APIs, giving developers a standardized way to extend the protocol with new application-specific data types \[89\], \[76\].

This architecture is especially relevant to OpenReel because video platforms depend on richer metadata than text-based social apps. A normal post, like, or follow record does not capture many of the fields a short-form video system needs. OpenReel will likely need metadata such as captions, tags, upload timestamps, duration, media references, and thumbnail references. It may also need richer engagement data such as watch duration, completion rate, rewatch behavior, or whether a video was watched with sound enabled. Without a structured way to represent this information, external services would have difficulty interoperating with the platform \[76\].

The main advantage of AT Protocol’s approach is portability. Metadata is attached to portable user records rather than locked inside a single platform’s private backend. This fits OpenReel’s goal of letting users move across providers without losing their content or account history, which is closely related to broader local-first and user-ownership principles in distributed systems design \[91\]. At the same time, there are tradeoffs. Richer metadata increases schema complexity, and behavioral metadata can raise privacy concerns if too much of it is exposed publicly. Research on decentralized social systems such as Mastodon shows that federated architectures can introduce operational and privacy challenges when data and metadata move across independently operated servers \[92\]. OpenReel will therefore need to distinguish between canonical metadata that should live in portable records and derived metadata that may be better handled by recommendation or moderation services.

### **4.6.4 Application to OpenReel**

OpenReel should use AT Protocol’s metadata model as its foundation and extend it through custom Lexicons under an “io.openreel.\*” namespace. Canonical metadata such as captions, tags, timestamps, media references, and other video descriptors should live in portable AT Protocol records so that content remains interoperable across providers. More advanced metadata, such as engagement aggregates or moderation labels, can then be consumed by recommendation and moderation services without requiring OpenReel to abandon the protocol’s decentralized structure \[89\], \[76\].

This approach gives OpenReel a clean way to support custom recommendation servers, moderation tools, and discovery systems while still keeping user content portable. Rather than storing all meaningful information in a centralized internal database, the platform can rely on structured metadata that other compliant services can read and build upon. That direction is more consistent with user-controlled decentralized systems than a design where metadata is trapped inside one provider’s private backend \[91\].

| Metadata Category | Storage / Schema |
| ----- | ----- |
| **Canonical metadata (portable records)** | io.openreel.video.post: caption, tags, videoRef (CID), duration, thumbnailRef, createdAt |
| **Engagement metadata (user-owned, opt-in)** | io.openreel.engagement.watchEvent: watchDuration, completionRate, soundEnabled, rewatchCount |
| **Aggregate signals (feed generators)** | io.openreel.engagement.completionRate: avgCompletionRate, totalViews, likeRatio, recency |
| **Content labels (moderation)** | AT Protocol label records: labelValue (nsfw, graphic-violence, safe), labelerDid, targetUri |

### **4.6.5 Conclusion**

Metadata is a core part of how a federated platform works, not just an implementation detail. AT Protocol already provides a strong structure for portable and interoperable metadata, but OpenReel will need to extend that structure for video-specific use cases. By defining custom Lexicons for video posts and related metadata, OpenReel can support recommendation, moderation, and discovery while still preserving portability, openness, and compatibility with the broader AT Protocol ecosystem \[76\], \[90\].

#   **5\. Design**

## **5.1 Overview**

The design of the OpenReel system defines the methodologies, architectural principles, and structural components used to implement a decentralized short-form video platform built on the AT Protocol. This section provides a high-level synthesis of how the system is organized and how its major components interact, establishing the foundation for the detailed design sections that follow.

OpenReel is designed as a federated, protocol-driven system that separates data ownership, computation, and presentation into distinct layers. At its core, the system leverages Personal Data Servers (PDSs) to give users control over their identity and data, while relying on independently deployable services for feed generation, moderation, and aggregation. This separation enables modularity, extensibility, and interoperability across the system.

The overall design follows a multi-tier architecture consisting of:

- Client interfaces (e.g., iOS application)  
- A decentralized data layer (PDS network)  
- Application services (feed generators, AppView, moderation)  
- Event streaming infrastructure (relay/firehose)  
- Media storage and delivery systems (cloud storage and CDN)

These components interact through standardized interfaces defined by the AT Protocol, including structured records, XRPC-based APIs, and real-time event streams. This approach eliminates tight coupling between services and enables independent development, deployment, and scaling of system components.

The remainder of Chapter 5 expands on this overview by detailing the following topics:

* System Architecture: Physical deployment topology, device access, and infrastructure

* Software Architecture: Logical service layers and data flow

* AT Protocol, Metadata, and APIs: Protocol mechanics, Lexicon schemas, and authentication

* Recommendation Algorithms: Feed curation strategies and engagement signals

* User Flow: Average user experience and UI/UX design

* Database, Video Storage, and Playback: Data persistence and media delivery

* Moderation System: Content safety and labeling architecture

* Open-Source and Interoperability: Extensibility and third-party integration

## **5.2 System Architecture**

### **5.2.1 Overview**

The OpenReel system architecture defines the deployment topology and interaction of physical and logical system components, including user devices, decentralized data servers, media infrastructure, and external compute services.

The system is designed as a federated, multi-tier architecture built on the AT Protocol, where:

- Users control identity and data via Personal Data Servers (PDSs)  
- Media delivery is offloaded to cloud storage and CDN infrastructure  
- Computation (including recommendation and moderation) occurs on independent services  
- Clients act as interchangeable interfaces to the network

OpenReel decentralizes data ownership but centralizes indexing and delivery for performance. This results in a decentralized and performant architecture, combining user-owned data with centralized acceleration layers for video delivery.

### **5.2.2 High-Level System Topology**

The system architecture consists of four primary tiers:

* Client Devices

* Decentralized Data Layer (PDS Network)

* Application Services Layer

* Media Storage and Delivery Layer

![][image3]  
*Figure: High-Level System Architecture Diagram.*

### **5.2.3 Component Descriptions**

#### **5.2.3.1 Client Devices**

Client devices represent the entrypoint to the system and include iOS smartphones (primary target) and potential future web, Android, desktop, or third-party clients.

| Responsibility | Description |
| ----- | ----- |
| **User interaction** | Full-screen video feed browsing, recording, and playback |
| **Video recording, uploading, and playback** | Native camera access, upload to PDS, HLS stream from CDN |
| **Feed selection** | Choose and swap between algorithm servers in real time |
| **Authentication via DID** | AT Protocol OAuth; identity not tied to any single server |

Clients are stateless with respect to identity ownership, relying on external PDSs for persistence.

#### **5.2.3.2 Personal Data Servers (PDSs)**

The PDS layer is the core feature of decentralization, where each user’s data is stored.

Each PDS:

- Stores user identity  
- Maintains content metadata and records  
- Hosts references to user-generated content

The key property is that users may self-host or use third-party providers, enabling portability and avoiding platform lock-in inherent in traditional social media platforms.

#### **5.2.3.3 Relay / Firehose Layer**

The relay layer aggregates updates across the decentralized network. Its responsibilities include:

- Collecting updates from multiple PDS nodes  
- Streaming data in real time  
- Providing a unified event stream, which is used by downstream feed generators

Relays may be operated by multiple providers, though OpenReel initially relies on a primary relay for aggregation.

#### **5.2.3.4 Application Services Layer**

This layer consists of independently deployable services that operate on top of the data layer. This layer includes AppView services for aggregation and moderation, as well as feed generation servers for ranking and recommending content.

These services process user data, but they do not own or manage it. They operate on metadata and signals. Additionally, they can be hosted by third parties, as this separation of concerns allows for greater modularity, algorithmic transparency, and extensibility.

#### **5.2.3.5 Media Storage and Delivery Layer**

Video delivery is handled through a hybrid centralized infrastructure optimized for performance. This component includes an AWS S3 bucket (independently associated with each PDS) for storing video assets and a Cloudflare Content Delivery Network (CDN) for high-speed, global distribution.

The core video data files are referenced by each PDS, which clients request from CDNs when handling video playback. This architecture uses existing cloud infrastructure to enable cheap and agile PDSs, as they need not store terabytes of video data.

#### **5.2.3.6 Device-to-Server Access**

The iOS client, targeting iPhone 12 and later on iOS 16 or higher, does not communicate with a single backend. During normal operation it maintains concurrent connections to several servers, each handling a distinct responsibility. All traffic runs over HTTPS using the AT Protocol's XRPC interface, a request-response format layered on standard HTTP verbs. No specialized networking stack is required, and the same pattern generalizes to any future web or Android client.

When the app launches, the client resolves the user's DID to locate their PDS, authenticates, and establishes a session. The PDS serves as the device's trusted anchor for identity and all user writes. Reads are retrieved from wherever the content resides. Video segments stream into AVPlayer over HLS from the CDN, feed data is requested from the user's selected AppView, and moderation labels are fetched from independent labeler services.

The result is that latency-sensitive operations route through the CDN, while identity-bound operations such as posting, deletion, and account migration route through the user's own PDS as the authoritative source of truth. A failure in any single server degrades but rarely halts the client, as a downed feed generator does not prevent playback of loaded segments or interaction with user-owned data.

### **5.2.4 End-to-End Data Flow**

#### **Video Upload Flow**

| Step | Actor | Action |
| ----- | ----- | ----- |
| 1 | User Device | Records a video using the native camera |
| 2 | iOS Client | Sends video file and metadata to the user's PDS |
| 3 | PDS | Transfers the video file to the associated S3 bucket |
| 4 | PDS | Saves metadata about the video to local storage |
| 5 | PDS | Sends an upload event to the Relay for processing |

#### **Feed Retrieval Flow**

| Step | Actor | Action |
| ----- | ----- | ----- |
| 1 | iOS Client | Navigates to For You page; selects a feed algorithm |
| 2 | Feed Generator | Processes available metadata and engagement signals |
| 3 | Feed Generator | Returns ranked list of content URIs to client |
| 4 | iOS Client | Renders feed; hydrates video metadata via AppView |

#### **Video Playback Flow**

| Step | Actor | Action |
| ----- | ----- | ----- |
| 1 | iOS Client | Requests video from CDN using its CID identifier |
| 2 | Cloudflare CDN | Serves HLS segments from local edge node |
| 3 | AVFoundation | Streams and renders content; manages audio session |

## 

## **5.3 Software Architecture**

The software architecture of OpenReel defines the logical structure and interaction of the system’s internal components, including how clients, protocol services, and application-level services communicate to support core functionality. While the system architecture (Section 5.2) describes where components are deployed and how they are physically organized, the software architecture focuses on how data flows through the system and how services coordinate behavior.

OpenReel adopts a protocol-driven, service-oriented architecture built on the AT Protocol. Rather than implementing a monolithic backend, the system is composed of loosely coupled services that interact through standardized interfaces and shared data abstractions. This approach aligns with the decentralized design goals of the platform, where data ownership, computation, and presentation and separated into distinct layers. Communication between these components is handled using the AT Protocol, which defines a consistent interface for reading, writing, and subscribing to data across the network. 

### **5.3.1 Architectural Layers**

To organize the design of OpenReel, the system is divided into logical architectural layers, each responsible for a distinct aspect of functionality. OpenReel consists of five primary layers:

| Layer | Components | Responsibility |
| ----- | ----- | ----- |
| 1\. Client Layer | iOS App, future web clients | User interface, video playback, recording, session state |
| 2\. Protocol Layer (AT Protocol) | XRPC, Lexicon, DID | Data structure, identity resolution, read/write interface, Relay streaming |
| 3\. Application Services Layer | Feed Generators, AppView | Recommendation, aggregation, moderation, content filtering |
| 4\. Event Stream Layer | Relay, Firehose | Coordinate between PDSs to propagate data to upstream services |
| 5\. Data Layer (PDS Network) | User Repositories, S3 storage | Identity storage, signed content records, portability |

![][image4]  
*Figure: Software layers diagram.*

#### **5.3.1.1 Client Layer**

The Client Layer consists of all frontend applications that interact with the OpenReel network, including the primary iOS application and any future web or third-party clients. This layer is responsible for:

- Rendering user interfaces  
- Handling user input (scrolling, uploading, selecting feeds)  
- Initiating requests for data  
- Managing playback and session state

Clients do not directly persist user data. Instead, they act as thin interfaces that communicate with backend components through the protocol layer. This design reduces the responsibilities of the clients by maintaining a clean client-server architecture.

#### **5.3.1.2 Protocol Layer**

The Protocol Layer provides the core communication and data abstraction framework for the system. It is implemented using the AT Protocol and associated Bluesky infrastructure. This layer is responsible for:

- Defining how data is structured and exchanged  
- Handling read/write interfaces between clients and servers  
- Streaming updates across the network via the Relay  
- Resolving user identities and routing requests

All interactions between clients and backend services pass through this layer. It acts as a standardized interface to ensure that different components can interoperate through abstract interfaces without requiring direct knowledge of each other’s internal implementations. This also allows our team to seamlessly work together by dividing work between services.

#### **5.3.1.3 Application Services Layer**

The Application Services Layer contains the core computational logic of the system, implemented as independent, loosely coupled services. This layer includes:

- Feed generation services  
- AppView services, including aggregation, filtering, and moderation  
- Potential third-party services like analytics and discovery

These services consume metadata and events from the protocol layer to perform transformations or computations on it. They then return structured results, such as ranked user feeds, to clients. The services in this layer do not own or persist user data. Instead, they operate on data provided through the protocol, enabling service independence and a strong separation of concerns.

#### **5.3.1.4 Event Stream Layer**

The Event Stream Layer provides the asynchronous communication backbone of the OpenReel system. It is implemented through the relay (firehose) and is responsible for distributing updates across all participating services. This layer is responsible for:

- Receiving update events from PDSs  
- Aggregating events into a unified, ordered stream  
- Broadcasting events to subscribed services such as feed generators and AppView  
- Enabling near real-time propagation of new content and metadata changes

When a user performs an action such as uploading a video or updating metadata, the change is first written to their PDS. The PDS then emits an event to the relay, which is exposed through the event stream. Application services subscribe to this stream and process events as they occur.

Unlike request-based interactions, which are initiated by clients, the Event Stream Layer operates in a push-based model, allowing services to react to changes without polling individual PDS instances. This reduces system overhead and avoids tight coupling between components.

#### **5.3.1.5 Data Layer**

The Data Layer is responsible for storing the user metadata in a network of PDSs, as well as the bulk video data in AWS S3 buckets. This layer is depended on by all other layers, and it serves as a source of truth regarding user accounts, video uploads, etc.

### **5.3.2 Software Architecture Diagram**

![][image5]  
*Figure: Software architecture diagram illustrating component interactions at runtime.*

### **5.3.3 Core Services and Responsibilities**

The OpenReel software architecture is composed of a set of core services, each responsible for a specific function within the system. These services operate independently and interact through the protocol layer, enabling a clear separation of concerns and allowing each component to evolve without tightly coupling to others.

#### **5.3.3.1 Personal Data Server (PDS)**

The Personal Data Server (PDS) is the primary non-video data storage component of the system and serves as the authoritative source of truth for user data. The PDS is responsible for:

- Storing user records, including video metadata and content references  
- Maintaining user identity and associated data  
- Handling read and write requests from clients  
- Publishing updates to the Relay when data changes

Each user is associated with a PDS, which ensures that their data is logically owned and managed independently of other system components. This design allows users to retain control over their data and enables portability across different service providers.

#### **5.3.3.2 Relay / Firehose**

The relay, also referred to as the firehose, provides a centralized event streaming mechanism that distributes updates across the system. The relay is responsible for:

- Receiving update events from PDS instances  
- Aggregating events into a unified stream  
- Broadcasting these events to subscribed services

This service enables an event-driven architecture, where downstream components do not need to directly query each PDS for updates. Instead, they can subscribe to the relay and react to changes in near real time. This significantly reduces coupling between components and improves scalability.

#### **5.3.3.3 Feed Generator Services**

Feed generator services are responsible for ranking and recommending content to users. They play the role of “the algorithm” in traditional social media platforms. These services:

- Consume metadata and event streams from the relay  
- Process data including content attributes and engagement data  
- Produce ranked lists of content identifiers

Feed generators are designed to be stateless and interchangeable, allowing multiple implementations to coexist. This enables experimentation with different recommendation strategies and allows users or clients to select which feed they want to consume.

#### **5.3.3.4 AppView Services**

AppView services provide an aggregation and filtering layer between backend services and client applications. The AppView layer is responsible for:

- Combining data from multiple sources  
- Filtering or moderating content before it reaches clients  
- Enriching responses with additional metadata  
- Shaping responses into formats suitable for client consumption

By centralizing these responsibilities, the AppView layer ensures that clients receive consistent, processed data without needing to implement complex logic locally. It also provides a natural integration point for moderation and policy enforcement.

### **5.3.4 Interfaces and APIs**

The OpenReel software architecture defines a set of concrete interfaces and APIs that govern how clients, data servers, and application services interact. These interfaces are designed to be consistent across components, enabling independent services to communicate without tight coupling. OpenReel uses a combination of:

- Request-based APIs for direct interactions  
- Structured records for shared data representation  
- Event streams for asynchronous updates

These are gateway-level application endpoints exposed by OpenReel for client simplicity. Internally, these requests are translated into AT Protocol-compatible record writes, reads, and feed queries.

#### **5.3.4.1 Client-to-Service Interactions**

Clients interact with the system through a set of HTTP-based API endpoints, routed through the NGINX API gateway. These endpoints support core user actions such as posting content, retrieving feeds, and fetching metadata.

Typical client requests include:

| Action | HTTP Endpoint |
| ----- | ----- |
| **Create post (upload metadata)** | POST /posts |
| **Get user posts** | GET /user/{did}/posts |
| **Fetch feed** | GET /feed?algorithm=for-you |
| **Fetch post** | GET /posts/{post\_id} |

These requests are routed internally to the appropriate service:

- Write operations \=\> Personal Data Server (PDS)  
- Feed requests \=\> Feed Generator  
- Content hydration \=\> AppView

This abstraction allows clients to operate against a single unified API, despite the underlying distributed architecture.

#### **5.3.4.2 Record-Based Data Model**

All persistent data is represented using structured records, which act as the primary data format shared across services. A typical video post record includes:

| {  "id": "post\_123",  "author": "did:example:123",  "caption": "Example video",  "video\_url": "https://cdn.openreel.com/video123.m3u8",  "created\_at": 1710000000} |
| :---- |

These records are stored in the user’s PDS, referenced by feed generators, and then retrieved and enriched by AppView services. This ensures that all services operate on a shared, consistent schema, reducing integration complexity.

#### **5.3.4.3 Event Stream Interface**

OpenReel uses an event-driven interface to propagate updates across the system. When a user creates a post:

1. The PDS stores the record  
2. The PDS emits an event to the relay  
3. The relay distributes the event to subscribed services

Example event:

| {  "type": "post\_created",  "post\_id": "post\_123",  "author": "did:example:123"} |
| :---- |

Feed generators and AppView subscribe to these events to update rankings, refresh cached data, and maintain consistency across services. This eliminates the need for PDS-based polling and enables near real-time updates to feeds and indexes.

#### **5.3.4.4 Pseudocode Example: Feed Request Flow**

For illustrative purposes, we include a pseudocode flow to show the interaction between the feed generator and the AppView. Since the feed generator is not responsible for posts themselves, they delegate this responsibility to the AppView, which is used by the clients to “hydrate” the posts, which describes the process of converting post IDs into full post metadata and video URLs. Then, the client will use these post objects to load videos from the CDN.

| def get\_user\_feed(user\_id):    \# Step 1: get ranked post IDs    post\_ids \= feed\_generator.get\_feed(user\_id)    \# Step 2: hydrate posts    posts \= appview.hydrate(post\_ids)    return posts |
| :---- |

#### **5.3.4.5 Software Architecture Summary**

The OpenReel software architecture organizes the system into a set of independent services with clearly defined responsibilities. Client applications handle user interaction, Personal Data Servers manage data storage, and application services such as feed generators and AppView handle computation and data processing.

These components communicate through consistent interfaces, allowing requests for data, feed generation, and content retrieval to be handled without direct coupling between services. The use of structured records ensures that all components operate on a shared data format, while event-driven updates allow services to stay synchronized without requiring constant polling.

Overall, this architecture separates storage, computation, and presentation into distinct parts of the system. This makes it easier to modify or extend individual components without affecting the rest of the system, and supports the system’s goal of operating as a decentralized, service-based platform.

## **5.4 AT Protocol Overview, Metadata, and API**

### **5.4.1 AT Protocol Overview**

The AT Protocol (Authenticated Transfer Protocol) is a decentralized, schema-driven architecture built for feed-based social media. Its main goal is to remove control over identity, content, and moderation from centralized corporations and return it to users and developers through a modular microservice design.

The core architecture consists of six components

| Component | Description |
| ----- | ----- |
| **Personal Data Server (PDS)** | Hosts user data, handles identity, content storage, and federation. Can be self-hosted or run by a third-party provider. |
| **Decentralized Identifier (DID)** | Cryptographic identifier that stays with the user even if they switch providers. Anchors identity independent of any server. |
| **Relay / Firehose** | Crawls all PDSes in the network and streams updates in real time. Feed generators and AppViews subscribe to this stream. |
| **AppView** | Indexes firehose data and serves the application API. Can implement invite systems, custom algorithms, and moderation strategies. |
| **Lexicon** | AT Protocol's schema definition language. Every record type and API endpoint is defined by a Lexicon schema in JSON, identified by a Namespaced Identifier (NSID). |
| **XRPC** | Cross-organizational Remote Procedure Calls: the HTTP-based request/response interface used for all AT Protocol API communication. |

### **5.4.2 Decentralized Identity (DIDs) and Authentication**

A user's handle resolves to a DID, which points to a document containing their public key and current PDS location. All changes to this document must be signed with the user's private key, creating a verifiable chain of updates. Users can change their handle without losing their identity because the DID remains constant.

AT Protocol implements a dual-key model:

* Signing key: used for daily operations (posting, liking, following)

* Recovery key: held by the user for account recovery

Authentication follows AT Protocol's OAuth standards. All data in transit is encrypted via HTTPS and secure WebSockets. On app launch, the iOS client resolves the user's DID, authenticates, and establishes a session token used for subsequent XRPC calls.

Credible Exit: When a user migrates their account, their full history, social graph, and identity keypair all transfer to the new PDS without data loss. Followers do not lose them, and all existing links to their content continue to work.

### 

### **5.4.3 Lexicon Schema System**

Lexicon is AT Protocol's schema definition language. Every record type and API endpoint is defined by a Lexicon schema written in JSON, identified by a Namespaced Identifier (NSID) in reverse-DNS format (e.g., app.bsky.feed.post). This is what enables different AT Protocol applications to understand each other's data.

Lexicon is designed to be extended: any developer who controls a domain can publish new Lexicons under their namespace without permission from Bluesky. OpenReel defines custom record types under the io.openreel.\* namespace:

| Lexicon NSID | Purpose | Key Fields |
| ----- | ----- | ----- |
| io.openreel.video.post | Core video post record | videoRef (CID), caption, tags\[\], duration, thumbnailRef, createdAt |
| io.openreel.engagement.watchEvent | Individual watch session (stored on user PDS) | videoUri, watchDuration, completionRate, soundEnabled, rewatchCount |
| io.openreel.engagement.completionRate | Anonymized aggregate signal for feed generators | videoUri, avgCompletionRate, totalViews, likeRatio, recency |
| io.openreel.feed.generator | Feed generator registration record | displayName, description, feedUri, rankingCriteria |

Design rules for OpenReel Lexicons:

* Fields use lowerCamelCase

* Use knownValues instead of closed enums for forward compatibility

* Design for forward compatibility, records stored across the network cannot be retroactively updated

### **5.4.4 Feed Generator API**

Feed generators are HTTP servers that receive a getFeedSkeleton request and return a list of post URIs. The requesting AppView handles hydration (fetching full record data). This means any developer can build and deploy a custom recommendation algorithm without access to OpenReel's infrastructure.

Pseudocode for getFeedSkeleton endpoint:

**POST /xrpc/app.bsky.feed.getFeedSkeleton**  
 Input:  { feed: string, cursor?: string, limit?: number }  
 Output: { feed: \[{ post: string }\], cursor?: string }

 **function getFeedSkeleton(params):**  
   signals \= fetchAggregateSignals(params.limit, params.cursor)  
   ranked \= rankByAlgorithm(signals)  // weights configurable per generator  
   return { feed: ranked.map(r \=\> ({ post: r.uri })), cursor: ranked.nextCursor }

### **5.4.5 Metadata Management Strategy**

AT Protocol manages metadata across three layers:

* Identity layer: DIDs and DID documents describing account ownership and public keys

* Repository layer: Signed personal repositories preserving integrity and portability

* Schema layer: Lexicon defines record structure and APIs

OpenReel distinguishes between two categories of metadata:

| Category | Description |
| ----- | ----- |
| **Canonical metadata** | Captions, tags, timestamps, media references, thumbnails, stored in portable AT Protocol records (io.openreel.video.post). Interoperable across providers. |
| **Derived / engagement metadata** | Anonymized aggregate signals (completion rates, view counts), consumed by recommendation and moderation services without being stored centrally. Individual behavioral data lives on the user's PDS. |

## 

## **5.5 Recommendation Algorithms**

A core architectural decision of OpenReel is how to design and support recommendation algorithms within a federated, user-controlled ecosystem. Unlike centralized platforms where a single opaque algorithm governs what every user sees, OpenReel's pluggable feed generator architecture allows users to choose between multiple transparent ranking systems.

### **5.5.1 Algorithm Families**

| Algorithm | Individual Data Required | Cold-Start Resilience | Transparency | Filter Bubble Risk |
| ----- | ----- | ----- | ----- | ----- |
| Chronological | None | Excellent | Perfect — ranked by timestamp | None |
| Heuristic / Popularity | None (aggregates only) | Good | High — weights are public | Moderate (popularity bias) |
| Collaborative Filtering | Shared engagement (opt-in) | Poor | Low–Moderate | Moderate |
| Content-Based Filtering | User's own history (local) | Moderate | High — explainable | High (filter bubble) |
| Social / Graph-Based | Follow graph (public) | Moderate | High | Moderate (echo chambers) |
| Hybrid | Varies by component | Good | Moderate | Manageable with exploration |

### **5.5.2 Engagement Signals**

The effectiveness of any recommendation algorithm depends on the quality of input signals. OpenReel's primary data-layer contribution is defining new Lexicon schemas that capture video-native engagement data currently absent from AT Protocol.

| Signal Type | Description |
| ----- | ----- |
| **Explicit signals** | Likes, reposts, follows, bookmarks, saves. Already exist as AT Protocol record types. High-confidence but sparse. |
| **Implicit behavioral signals** | Watch duration, completion rate, rewatch count, sound enabled, scroll-past speed, pause events. Stored on user's PDS. Available to feed generators only with user opt-in. |
| **Content metadata** | Tags, captions, audio track IDs, creator ID, video duration, upload timestamp, content safety labels. Attached to video record; available to all feed generators. |
| **Social graph data** | Public follow graph and second-order connections. Available via AT Protocol firehose; no additional data collection required. |

### **5.5.3 Tiered Algorithm Strategy**

#### **Tier 1: Default Feeds (No Individual Data Required)**

* Chronological Feed: Recent posts from followed accounts; transparent baseline

* Trending / Heuristic Feed: Weighted combination of aggregate signals. Formula: 0.4 × completion rate \+ 0.3 × like ratio \+ 0.3 × recency decay. Weights publicly displayed.

* Social Discovery Feed: Videos from creators within the user's extended follow graph

#### **Tier 2: Opt-In Personalized Feeds**

* Content-Based Feed: Recommends videos matching demonstrated interests; uses engagement history against content metadata

* Collaborative Filtering Feed: Leverages cross-user behavioral patterns to surface novel content

* Hybrid Feed: Combines content-based and collaborative filtering with an exploration component for new-creator visibility

#### **Tier 3: Third-Party Feed Generators**

Any developer can build and deploy a custom recommendation algorithm by implementing the getFeedSkeleton API. OpenReel's contribution is extending this model with video-specific engagement data via new Lexicon schemas, so that third-party generators can access richer signals than text-based social feeds currently provide.

### **5.5.4 Recommendation Algorithm Pseudocode**

**// Heuristic Feed Algorithm (Tier 1: no opt-in required)**  
 function scoreVideo(video, aggregates):  
   completionScore \= aggregates\[video.uri\].avgCompletionRate  // 0.0–1.0  
   likeScore      \= aggregates\[video.uri\].likeRatio           // likes / views  
   recencyScore   \= exp(-lambda \* hoursAgo(video.createdAt))  // decay function  
   return 0.4 \* completionScore \+ 0.3 \* likeScore \+ 0.3 \* recencyScore

  **// Collaborative Filtering Feed (Tier 2: requires opt-in)**  
 function getCollaborativeRecs(userId, watchHistory, limit):  
   similarUsers \= findSimilarUsers(userId, watchHistory)   // cosine similarity on watch vectors  
   candidates   \= union(watchedVideos(similarUsers)) \- watchedVideos(userId)  
   return rankByEngagementAmongSimilarUsers(candidates, similarUsers, limit)

## **5.6 User Flow**

### **5.6.1 Average User Experience in a Decentralized System**

To a user who has never heard of the AT Protocol, OpenReel should feel like any other short-form video app. Onboarding starts with PDS selection, but the default option is the OpenReel-hosted PDS, and a new user can accept it and move on in a single tap. DID-based authentication is presented as a normal login. After account creation the user lands on the video feed, and HLS playback through AVFoundation runs in the background without the user needing to know what either of those things are. Recording, posting, following, and scrolling all work the way a user would expect. Uploads go through the moderation pipeline before they appear in feeds, which shows up as a short processing delay on new posts and nothing else. Federation does not come up anywhere in the core experience. 

The decentralized parts of the system are there, but a user has to look for them. The feed defaults to three tabs, Following, For You, and Trending, and none of them require the user to share engagement data. The Feed Stats tab shows that each of these is a separate feed generator, and the user can swap between them or pick a third-party generator without touching their account. If the user picks a personalized feed, a confirmation sheet explains what engagement data gets shared with that specific generator. Without an opt-in, watch behavior stays on the user’s PDS. The profile tab holds the options in that matter for platform control: account migration to another PDS, which carries the user’s handle, posts, and social graph through AT Protocol’s credible exit mechanism, and labeler subscription management, which lets the user stack additional moderation services on top of OpenReel’s baseline.

Moderation in OpenReel operates at the reach layer, not the storage layer. A video that gets labeled or held back from feeds still lives on the user’s PDS. For the large majority of users who never open any of these settings, decentralization is just a guarantee running in the background. Their identity, content, and engagement data sit on a server tied to them rather than on one owned by a company, and that holds whether they ever take advantage of it or not.

### **5.6.2 User Flow Table: Onboarding**

| Step | Screen / Action | System Behavior |
| ----- | ----- | ----- |
| 1 | App Launch | Resolve DID if previously authenticated; otherwise show onboarding |
| 2 | PDS Selection | Default: OpenReel hosted PDS (one tap). Optional: enter custom PDS URL |
| 3 | Account Creation / Login | Create new DID or authenticate with existing one via AT Protocol OAuth |
| 4 | Feed Landing | Load default chronological feed of followed accounts (no data required) |
| 5 | Explore / For You | Trending heuristic feed powered by aggregate signals |

### **5.6.3 User Flow Table: Video Upload**

| Step | Screen / Action | System Behavior |
| ----- | ----- | ----- |
| 1 | Tap Record | Access device camera and microphone; begin recording |
| 2 | Stop & Preview | Preview recorded clip; option to re-record |
| 3 | Add Metadata | Enter caption, add tags; set visibility |
| 4 | Publish | Content classification check (automated). If approved: upload to PDS → S3; emit event to Relay |
| 5 | Confirmation | Video appears in creator's profile and enters feed discovery |

## **5.7 Database, Video Storage, and Playback**

### **5.7.1 Database Strategy**

OpenReel does not use a single centralized database that must be deployed on every server. Such an approach would create compatibility issues across independently operated PDS instances and undermine the decentralized design goals.

Instead, OpenReel's data persistence is distributed across purpose-appropriate storage systems:

| Storage System | Purpose |
| ----- | ----- |
| **PDS (SQLite, per instance)** | Each PDS runs its own lightweight SQLite database storing user identity records, AT Protocol repository data, and references to media blobs. Self-contained per user; portable. |
| **AppView (PostgreSQL)** | The AppView maintains a centralized index of content metadata for efficient querying, feed generation, and search. This is read-optimized and regularly synced from the Relay firehose. |
| **Feed Generator (PostgreSQL)** | Stores anonymized aggregate engagement signals (completion rates, view counts, like ratios) used for ranking. No individual behavioral data. |
| **Metadata Archive (Centralized)** | A centralized metadata store for cross-service queries, moderation audit logs, and content labeling records. Managed by OpenReel; does not store raw video or personal PDS data. |
| **Redis (Cache)** | Caches hot feed results, session tokens, and frequently accessed metadata to reduce database load and improve API response times. |

### **5.7.2 Entity Relationship Diagram (ERD)**

![][image8]

*Figure: Entity Relationship Diagram (ERD) for the OpenReel metadata database.*

### **5.7.3 Video Storage Architecture**

Video delivery in OpenReel separates blob storage (ownership) from media delivery (performance):

* Blob storage: Video files are stored as blobs on the user's PDS, referenced by a Content Identifier (CID). The PDS is the authoritative, portable source of truth.

* S3 origin: Each PDS is associated with an AWS S3 bucket. The PDS transfers uploaded video blobs to S3 for durable, scalable storage.

* Cloudflare CDN: The AppView serves video to users via CloudFront, which caches HLS segments at edge locations. PDS stays lightweight; CDN handles the performance-sensitive delivery.

### **5.7.4 Video Encoding and Streaming Protocol**

| Component | Selection |
| ----- | ----- |
| **Streaming Protocol** | HLS (HTTP Live Streaming): native iOS support, adaptive bitrate, no third-party dependencies |
| **Primary Codec** | H.265 (HEVC): 40–50% smaller files than H.264; native recording format on iPhones in target range (iPhone 12+) |
| **Fallback Codec** | H.264 (AVC): universal compatibility fallback for edge cases |
| **Future Codec** | AV1: planned when hardware decode support expands beyond iPhone 15 Pro (A17 Pro+) |
| **iOS Playback** | AVFoundation / AVPlayer \+ AVQueuePlayer for sequential feed playback |
| **Segment Length** | 2–6 seconds per HLS segment for smooth ABR switching |

### **5.7.5 Buffering and Preloading Strategy**

OpenReel implements directional preloading for the vertical feed:

* Preload 2–3 videos ahead: Initialize AVPlayerItems for the next clips in the feed as soon as the current video starts playing, triggering HLS manifest and first segment fetches in the background without starting audio

* Release behind: Videos behind the current scroll position have their AVPlayerItem set to nil to release memory

* Audio bleed prevention: The outgoing AVPlayer is explicitly paused and its audio session deactivated before activating the incoming one, directly addressing the audio bleed issue documented in existing AT Protocol video apps

**// Directional preloading pseudocode**  
 function onVideoStarted(currentIndex, feedItems):  
   // Preload next 2 videos  
   for i in (currentIndex+1)..(currentIndex+3):  
     if playerPool\[i\] \== nil:  
       playerPool\[i\] \= AVPlayerItem(url: feedItems\[i\].hlsURL)

    // Release old videos (2+ behind current)  
   for i in 0..(currentIndex-2):  
     playerPool\[i\] \= nil  // releases memory

  function onSwipeToNext(outgoingPlayer, incomingPlayer):  
   outgoingPlayer.pause()  
   AVAudioSession.sharedInstance().setActive(false)  // prevent audio bleed  
   incomingPlayer.play()  
   AVAudioSession.sharedInstance().setActive(true)

## **5.8 Moderation System**

One of the most consequential design challenges in building OpenReel is determining how to handle content moderation in a federated environment. A decentralized platform distributes content hosting and identity across many independent servers, making it impossible to rely on any single actor to police the entire network. At the same time, a platform without meaningful moderation is just as likely to become a venue for harmful content as any centralized alternative.

OpenReel adopts Bluesky's open labeling architecture as its moderation foundation, extended with automated classification capabilities suited to video content. The system operates on three stackable layers.

### **5.8.1 Moderation Architecture: Three Layers**

#### **Layer 1: Baseline Platform Moderation**

OpenReel hardcodes a default labeling service that enforces minimum community standards across the entire network. This baseline covers:

* Clearly illegal content: child sexual abuse material (CSAM) using perceptual hash-matching against known hash databases as an automated first pass

* Content promoting real-world violence

* Safe-by-default feeds: video must pass automated classification before entering discovery

#### **Layer 2: Open Labeler Ecosystem**

Following Bluesky's model, OpenReel supports independent labeling services that any organization, community, or developer can operate. Labelers annotate video content with standardized labels that client applications interpret and surface to users. This allows specialized communities (e.g., children's content platforms) to apply stricter filtering without requiring changes to the underlying platform.

| Label Value | Client Behavior |
| ----- | ----- |
| **nsfw** | Adult content — blurred by default; hidden from minors |
| **graphic-violence** | Violent imagery — blurred with content warning |
| **misinformation** | Flagged for review — annotated with warning label |
| **spam** | Spam or low-quality content — deprioritized in feeds |
| **safe** | Cleared by automated classification — eligible for discovery |

#### **Layer 3: Automated Classification Pipeline**

OpenReel provides a reference implementation of a video classification pipeline that PDS operators and labelers can optionally deploy. This pipeline applies frame-level and audio-level classifiers to uploaded videos, generating candidate labels that human moderators review and confirm via an Ozone-compatible interface.

Automated techniques used:

* Perceptual hash-matching: Detects known illegal or harmful content; near-duplicate detection for re-encoded evasion attempts

* ML frame-level classifiers: Flag harmful visual content; trained on labeled instances using supervised learning

* Audio transcript classifiers: Flag harmful speech, hate speech, and misinformation in spoken content

* Federated learning (future): PDS operators may share model parameters to improve classification quality without pooling raw video data. Research shows federated approaches improve harmful content detection by 12.69% vs. purely local models.

### **5.8.2 Moderation Decision Table**

| Stage | Actor | Decision / Action |
| ----- | ----- | ----- |
| Upload | iOS Client / PDS | Video submitted → queue for automated classification |
| Automated scan | Classification pipeline | Hash match? → immediate block. ML score \> threshold? → flag for review. Else → label 'safe' |
| Human review queue | OpenReel moderation team / labeler | Flagged content reviewed. Confirm label or clear. Escalate if needed. |
| Label published | Labeler service | Label written to AT Protocol; AppView reads label; feed generators exclude or deprioritize |
| User appeal | User → Labeler | Creator can appeal; labeler reviews; moderation is not a single removal authority: creator content still exists on their PDS even if one AppView chooses not to surface it |

### **5.8.3 Comparison with Mastodon and Bluesky Moderation Approaches**

| Dimension | Mastodon | Bluesky | OpenReel |
| ----- | ----- | ----- | ----- |
| Authority | Instance admins | Independent labelers | Baseline platform \+ open labelers |
| Scope | Local per instance | Network-wide per labeler | Network-wide (baseline) \+ community |
| Consistency | Inconsistent | Consistent per labeler | Consistent baseline; variable above |
| Automated tools | Minimal | Ozone (human-driven) | Automated pipeline \+ Ozone review |
| User control | Limited | Choose labelers | Choose labelers on top of baseline |

## **5.9 Open-Source and Interoperability**

OpenReel is designed from the ground up to be extensible. By building on the AT Protocol and following open standards, the platform enables independent developers, researchers, and operators to plug into the network, build on top of it, and even replace individual components with their own implementations.

### **5.9.1 What Open-Sourcing Provides**

| Capability | Value Provided |
| ----- | ----- |
| **Algorithmic transparency** | Anyone can inspect the default feed ranking code, verify that weights are what the UI claims, and audit for bias. This is the antidote to opaque algorithmic black boxes. |
| **Third-party feed generators** | Any developer implementing the getFeedSkeleton API can deploy a custom algorithm and have users select it in the app. 50,000+ community-built algorithms already exist in the Bluesky ecosystem. |
| **Alternative frontends** | Because the media and algorithm layers communicate through open protocol interfaces, the iOS app is one possible client. Third-party developers can build web apps, Android clients, or specialized interfaces. |
| **Self-hosted PDS** | Users and operators can run their own PDS instance. OpenReel's architecture is PDS-agnostic: any AT Protocol-compliant server works. |
| **Custom moderation labelers** | Organizations can deploy their own Ozone-compatible labeling service with custom policies. |
| **Research and experimentation** | The platform provides an open environment for researchers and students to design and test new recommendation algorithms on real-world data. |

### **5.9.2 Configuration Steps: Starting an OpenReel Server**

The following describes the high-level server configuration process at a conceptual level. Detailed step-by-step deployment instructions are not in scope for this design document but will be documented separately.

| Step | Component | Description |
| ----- | ----- | ----- |
| 1 | Provision server | Acquire a cloud VM or dedicated server (minimum: 2 vCPU, 4 GB RAM, 50 GB SSD for PDS). AWS EC2, DigitalOcean, or equivalent. |
| 2 | Configure domain | Register a domain for the PDS handle (e.g., pds.yourdomain.com). Set up DNS A record pointing to server IP. |
| 3 | Deploy PDS | Clone the AT Protocol PDS reference implementation. Configure environment variables (domain, admin password, blob storage endpoint). Start the PDS service. |
| 4 | Configure S3 bucket | Create an AWS S3 bucket for blob storage. Set bucket policy, configure CORS, and link bucket credentials to PDS config. |
| 5 | Configure CDN | Set up Cloudflare or CloudFront in front of the S3 bucket. Configure cache rules for HLS segments (short TTL) and thumbnails (long TTL). |
| 6 | Deploy AppView | Clone and configure the OpenReel AppView service. Point it at the Relay endpoint to begin indexing the firehose. |
| 7 | Deploy Feed Generator | Clone the default feed generator implementation. Configure algorithm weights and data access endpoints. Register the feed generator DID. |
| 8 | Deploy classification pipeline | Optional: deploy the automated content classification service. Configure ML model endpoints and label thresholds. |
| 9 | Launch and verify | Confirm end-to-end: create a test account, upload a video, verify it appears in feed, verify label pipeline runs, verify CDN serves HLS segments. |

### **5.9.3 Flexibility of the Open Model**

Users are never locked into any single component of OpenReel's stack. The separation of concerns means:

* A user who dislikes the default feed algorithm can subscribe to a third-party one

* A user who distrusts the hosted PDS can migrate to a self-hosted or community-operated one

* A creator flagged by one AppView's moderation still has their content on their PDS: it is not deleted, merely not surfaced by that particular viewing service

* A developer who wants to build a competing frontend simply implements the AT Protocol client interfaces

This modularity is what makes OpenReel a platform template rather than a walled garden. The iOS app is a reference implementation; the architecture is the product.

#   **6\. Development Plan**

## **6.1 Development Strategy**

OpenReel follows an Agile development strategy using two-week sprint cycles, selected over Waterfall and Spiral for reasons tied to the project's architecture and team structure.

The AT Protocol architecture imposes a strict dependency chain: the PDS must be operational before the relay can aggregate events, the relay must stream before the AppView can index, and the AppView must serve data before feed generators or the iOS client can be tested end-to-end. Each component represents a natural sprint boundary where the team validates integration with everything built before it.

A Waterfall approach would defer integration risk to the end of the semester when recovery time is shortest. Several design decisions, including classifier library selection, HLS preloading behavior, and engagement schema design, benefit from iterative refinement against working software rather than upfront specification. Two-week sprints with weekly syncs keep cross-domain interfaces aligned, ensuring backend changes surface quickly on the client side rather than being discovered late.

Work is tracked on a shared board and each sprint concludes with a working demo and a short retrospective. The sprint sequence follows the system's build order:

* Early sprints: deploy PDS, relay, and AppView; confirm end-to-end event flow

* Middle sprints: implement authentication, feed retrieval, and video playback on iOS

* Later sprints: layer on content classification, feed generator integration, and upload flow

This ordering ensures each sprint builds on validated infrastructure and the most integration-sensitive components are tested against real services rather than mocks.

## **6.2 Development Timeline**

Development will take place from August to December 2026, over roughly 16 weeks. With six team members, the team will coordinate across different areas of the project to avoid being blocked by a single development task. Two-week sprints align well within four-week blocks. At the start of each sprint, tasks are divided into actionable tickets based on respective team roles.

| Implement opt-in personalized and collaborative filtering feeds | Andrew Hellman | 8 |
