| Build open-source plugin architecture for third-party developers | Hamza Farah, Zack Murry | 8 |
| Create public landing page, documentation, and self-hosting guide | All | 8 |
| Final end-to-end testing and retrospective | All | 8 |

## **6.3 Work Delegation**

The OpenReel team consists of six engineers, each assigned to areas of the project that align with their technical strengths and academic backgrounds. The following outlines each team member's primary responsibilities throughout the development of the system.

Zack Murry serves as a Backend Engineer with a focus on system and software architecture. Zack is responsible for designing the overall system topology, including the decentralized multi-tier architecture built on the AT Protocol. His work covers the interaction between client devices, Personal Data Servers, the Relay/Firehose layer, the Application Services Layer, and the Media Storage and Delivery Layer. Zack also leads the software architecture design, defining how the Client, Protocol, Application Services, and Media Interface layers interact at runtime.

Andrew Hellman serves as a Backend Engineer with a focus on recommendation algorithms and data architecture. Andrew is responsible for building OpenReel's tiered recommendation system, including the default chronological, heuristic, and social discovery feeds, as well as the opt-in personalized and collaborative filtering feeds. He also oversees the definition of custom Lexicon schemas for video-specific engagement signals such as watch duration and completion rate, and leads the design of the centralized metadata architecture.

Gabe Edwards serves as a Front End Engineer with a focus on user interface design and content moderation. Gabe is responsible for building the iOS user interface, including the sign-in experience, video feed, and video playback views. He also leads the implementation of OpenReel's three-layer moderation system across the platform.

Hamza Farah serves as a Full Stack Engineer with a focus on Swift development and iOS infrastructure. Hamza is responsible for the native iOS implementation of the application, including video recording, playback using AVFoundation, and the feed interface. He also handles server configuration and deployment, bringing the system from bare infrastructure to a running deployment, as well as the open-source plugin architecture that allows third-party developers to extend the platform.

Hao (Joe) Ouyang serves as a Full Stack Engineer with a focus on front end and backend integration. Joe is responsible for bridging the iOS client with backend protocol services, handling the integration of the AT Protocol's XRPC interface with the application layer. He also leads the implementation of the database design, video storage architecture, and ensures the system meets performance, security, and compatibility requirements.

Jersey Schunk serves as a Full Stack Engineer with a focus on front end and backend integration. Jersey is responsible for implementing the device-to-server access model, ensuring the iOS client communicates correctly with the PDS, CDN, AppView, and labeler services during normal operation. Jersey also leads the work on making the decentralized system feel seamless and intuitive for end users who have no prior knowledge of federation or the AT Protocol.

## **6.4 Contingency Planning**

#### **AT Protocol API Changes or Instability**

OpenReel depends on the AT Protocol for identity resolution, PDS communication, and XRPC calls. If Bluesky introduces breaking changes to its lexicon schemas or XRPC endpoints mid development, the team will pin the app to the last stable version of the AT Protocol SDK and defer migration until affected features can be isolated and retested. If the protocol’s public infrastructure becomes temporarily unreachable during development, a local mock PDS will be used to simulate responses so that iOS client work and APPView integration can continue without blocking an entire sprint

#### **Video Delivery Pipeline Failure**

The video pipeline stores uploads in S3 based object storage and delivers them through a CDN using HLS segments, with AVFoundation handling playback on the client. If the CDN experiences outages or rate limiting during development, the team will serve video directly from the origin storage bucket, accepting higher latency as a temporary tradeoff for unblocked testing. If adaptive bitrate encoding or HLS segment generation introduces more integration complexity than a single spring can absorb, the team will ship single resolution MP4 playback through AVPlayer first and layer in multi bitrate HLS once the core feed and playback loop is validated. 

#### **PDS Federation Scoping**	

User content and identity records live on Personal Data Servers, and the relay aggregates events across PDS instances for the AppView to index. If syncing content across multiple PDS instances introduces data consistency problems or latency that disrupts the sprint demo cycle, the team will scope the MVP to a single hosted PDS instance. This preserves the upload and watch loop, DID based authentication, and XRPC communication while deferring full multi-PDS federation to a later phase. 

#### **Content Classification Bottleneck**

OpenReel’s architecture classifies videos before they reach any feed generator. If the classification pipeline introduces processing delays that bottleneck the upload flow, or if the selected classifier library underperforms on accuracy during integration sprints, the team will implement a simplified pass / fail filter using basic metadata checks as a stopgap. The full classification model can then be refined once the feed retrieval and playback path is stable end to end. 

#### **Build Environment and Distribution**

Development targets iOS 15 on iPhone 12 and later. If Xcode version mismatches or provisioning profile conflicts block builds across the six person team, the group will standardize on a pinned Xcode version and a shared provisioning profile. TestFlight will handle all external distribution to avoid App Store review bottlenecks during active sprint cycles.

#### **Team Member Availability**

If a team member becomes unavailable, their in progress tasks will be redistributed based on familiarity with the relevant codebase area. All work lives in a shared repository with documented branches, so any member can pick up incomplete work without significant ramp up. The two week sprint structure and weekly syncs described in section 6.1 ensure reassignment decisions surface quickly rather than stalling progress. 

#### **Feed Generator Failure**

OpenReel's feed experience depends on feed generator servers returning ranked lists of content to the client. If a feed generator becomes unreachable or returns malformed responses during development, the team will fall back to a chronological feed pulled directly from the PDS firehose, requiring no feed generator at all. This fallback will preserve the core upload, playback, and scrolling loop so that the iOS client development and AppView integration can continue unblocked. If feed generator latency consistently exceeds the acceptable thresholds during sprint demos, then the team will cache the last known feed skeleton on the client and serve stale results rather than blocking the UI on a slow response.

## **6.5 Road Blocks**

The following known architectural challenges have been identified in advance. Each represents a real limitation of building a video-first platform on the AT Protocol, which was designed primarily for text-based social media.

1. **Video is Non-Native to AT Protocol.** The protocol is fundamentally built around text-based lexicons (like app.bsky.feed.post). There's no official video record type in the core protocol, so our team would need to define custom lexicons, which may not be recognized or federated by other PDS implementations.  
2. **Blob Storage Limitations.** AT Protocol handles media as "blobs" uploaded to a PDS, but a PDS is not a CDN. Storing and serving video files (even short ones at several MB each) through a personal data server creates serious scalability and bandwidth problems. There's no native video streaming layer baked into the protocol.  
3. **No Native Video Streaming Support.** The protocol has no concept of adaptive bitrate streaming (HLS/DASH), seek support, or buffering. our team would need to build an entirely separate video delivery pipeline (likely a third-party CDN or object storage like S3) and then just reference URLs in your AT Protocol records,  which partially defeats the decentralization model.  
4. **Federation and Relay Compatibility.** The BGS (Big Graph Service / Relay) that syncs records across the network is optimized for lightweight lexicon records. Video-heavy firehose traffic could get filtered out, rate-limited, or simply ignored by relays that aren't configured to handle our own custom lexicons.  
5. **DID and Identity Portability vs. Video Hosting.** AT Protocol's core promise is that users own their data and can migrate between PDS hosts. But video files are large, if a user migrates their PDS, their video blobs need to migrate too. There's no defined migration path for large binary assets in the protocol today.  
6. **Moderation Infrastructure.** Bluesky's Ozone moderation tooling is built around text posts and images. Moderating video content (takedowns, CSAM detection, copyright claims) has no defined path in the current AT Protocol ecosystem, which is a significant legal and safety roadblock.  
7. **Lack of Community/Ecosystem Precedent.** Unlike building on top of ActivityPub (where PeerTube already solved video federation), AT Protocol has no existing video implementation to reference. Your team would be pioneering without a map, which multiplies the risk of every other roadblock above.
